, hence it has only locally planar singularities. The Hitchin base
\mathcal{A}
admits three notable open subsets
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{sm}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{ell}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{reg}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, called respectively the smooth locus, the elliptic locus and the regular locus, defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{sm}}

\displaystyle:=\{{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}:X_{\underline{a}}\textrm{ is smooth and connected}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{ell}}

\displaystyle:=\{{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}:X_{\underline{a}}\textrm{ is integral}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{reg}}

\displaystyle:=\{{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}:X_{\underline{a}}\textrm{ is reduced and connected}\}.

We come now to the spectral correspondence for Higgs pairs. The following spectral correspondence is due to
[
Sch98
]
,
[
CL16
]
and
[
dC17
]
, as a generalization of the spectral correspondence stated in
[
BNR89
]
for smooth characteristics.

Proposition 1.4.4
.

(Spectral correspondence)

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}(r)
be any characteristic and let
X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\underline{a}}}C
be the associated spectral curve. Let
\mathcal{O}_{C}(1)
be an ample line bundle on
C
; let
\mathcal{O}_{X_{\underline{a}}}(1):=\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{C}(1)\big)
be the ample line bundle on
X_{\underline{a}}
obtained by pullback and denote with
H
the associated polarization on
X_{\underline{a}}
.

(1)

For any integer
d
, there is an isomorphism of stacks:

\ \mathcal{H}_{r,d}^{-1}({\underline{a}})\xrightarrow[\Pi]{\hskip 8.19447pt{}_{\sim}\hskip 8.19447pt}\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X_{\underline{a}},d^{\prime}),

where
d^{\prime}=d+r(1-g)-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X_{\underline{a}}})=d+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\ell
and
g=g(C)
is the genus of
C
. If
\mathcal{M}
is a torsion-free sheaf of rank 1 on
X_{\underline{a}}
, then
\Pi(\mathcal{M}):=(E,\Phi)
where

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\cdot x):\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow L\otimes\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})\simeq\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L\otimes\mathcal{M}).

(2)

A torsion-free sheaf of rank 1
\mathcal{M}
on
X_{\underline{a}}
is
H
-semistable if and only if the associated Higgs pair
\Pi(\mathcal{M})
is semistable on
C
. Hence, the above correspondence yields an isomorphism of schemes:

H_{r,d}^{-1}({\underline{a}})\simeq\overline{J}(X_{\underline{a}},H,d^{\prime}).

Finally, in the following proposition, inspired by
[
BNR89
, Remark 3.7]
, we show that the torsion-free sheaf
\mathcal{M}
corresponding to an Higgs pair
(E,\Phi)
can be always resumed, up to twisting, as the eigenspace of
\pi^{*}\Phi
with eigenvalue
x
.

Proposition 1.4.5
.

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}(r)
be any characteristic and let
X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\underline{a}}}C
be the associated spectral curve. Let
\mathcal{M}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X_{\underline{a}},d^{\prime})
be a torsion-free rank-1 sheaf on
X_{\underline{a}}
corresponding to the Higgs pair
(E,\Phi)\in\mathcal{H}_{r,d}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
on
C
. Then, the following exact sequence holds:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(L^{1-r})\rightarrow\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E\xrightarrow{\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(\Phi)-x}\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L\xrightarrow{\ev}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L\rightarrow 0

where
\ev
is induced by the evaluation map
\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

We first prove the exact sequence locally. Let
U=\operatorname{Spec}R\subseteq C
be any open affine subset where
L
is trivial.
The preimage
V=\pi^{-1}(U)
is then equal to
\operatorname{Spec}(R[x]/P(x))
where
P(x)=x^{r}+a_{1}x^{r-1}+\dots+a_{r-1}x+a_{r}
and the
a_{i}
’s denote (with a slight abuse of notation) the elements in
R
corresponding to the entries of
{\underline{a}}
after choosing the trivialization of
L
. Denote
R[x]/P(x)=S
and note that the inclusion
R\subset S
makes
S
into an
R
-module with basis
1,x,\dots,x^{r-1}
.

Recall that
E=\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})
and
\Phi=\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\cdot x)
. Denote with
M
the
S
-module corresponding to
\mathcal{M}
on
V=\operatorname{Spec}S
. The pushforward
\pi_{{\underline{a}},*}(\mathcal{M})
corresponds on
V
to the restriction of scalars of
M
from
S
to
R
. Then,
\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E
correspond on
V
to the
S
-module
S\otimes_{R}M
, with the structure of
S
-module given by multiplication on the left-hand side of the tensor product. On the other hand, any element
p(x)\in S
acts on
S\otimes_{R}M
also on the right-hand side. We denote such action as

\displaystyle\overline{p(x)}:S\otimes_{R}M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow S\otimes_{R}M

\displaystyle q(x)\otimes m

\displaystyle\longmapsto q(x)\otimes p(x)\cdot m.

Regarding the evaluation map, it can be defined at the level of
S
-modules as

\displaystyle\ev:S\otimes_{R}M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow M

\displaystyle p(x)\otimes m

\displaystyle\longmapsto p(x)\cdot m.

With this notation, we are left to prove that the following exact sequence of
S
-modules holds:

0\rightarrow M\rightarrow S\otimes_{R}M\xrightarrow{\Psi=\overline{x}-x}S\otimes_{R}M\xrightarrow{\ev}M\rightarrow 0.

The proof of the exactness on the right follows exactly the proof of
[
Bou89
, III 10, Proposition 18]
, with the difference that
S\otimes_{R}M
has a finite
S
-basis.

To prove the exactness on the left, first consider the following morphism of
R
-modules:

\displaystyle Q=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}\overline{x}^{r-1-i-j}\overline{a}_{j}:M

\displaystyle\longrightarrow S\otimes_{R}M

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)

where we put
a_{0}=1
. We prove first that
\Psi\circ Q=0
. Let
m\in M
be any element. If we compute
x\cdot Q(m)
, recalling that
x^{r}=-\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}a_{r-i}x^{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle x\cdot Q(m)

\displaystyle=x\cdot\left(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)\right)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{r-2}x^{i+1}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)-\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\left(a_{r-i}x^{i}\right)\otimes m.

Changing
i+1
with
i
in the first sum and moving the
a_{r-i}
by linearity, we obtain

\displaystyle x\cdot Q(m)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(x^{r-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)-\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes(a_{r-i}\cdot m)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-i-1}x^{r-i-j}a_{j}+a_{r-i}\right)\cdot m-1\otimes(a_{r}\cdot m)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-i-1}(x^{r-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)-1\otimes(a_{r}\cdot m).

Finally, recalling that
-a_{r}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}x^{r-j}a_{j}
, we can write

x\cdot Q(m)=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-i-1}(x^{r-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right).

On the other hand, the computation of
\overline{x}(Q(m))
gives

\displaystyle\overline{x}(Q(m))

\displaystyle=\overline{x}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)\right)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right).

We conclude that
\Psi(Q(m))=\overline{x}(Q(m))-x\cdot Q(m)=0
for arbitrary
m\in M
; then
\Psi\circ Q=0
.

We can now prove that
Q
is a morphism of
S
-modules. Since
Q
is
R
-linear by definition, it remains only to show that
Q(x\cdot m)=x\cdot Q(m)
; but it is clear by the definition of
\overline{x}
that
Q(x\cdot m)=\overline{x}(Q(m))
, which is equal to
x\cdot Q(m)
by the previous computation.

Note also that
Q
is injective. Indeed, after choosing the monomials
1,x,\dots,x^{r-1}
as a
R
-basis for
S
, we have a canonical isomorphism
S\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{r-1}Rx^{i}
that induces an isomorphism
S\otimes_{R}M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{r-1}(x^{i}\otimes M)
. To see that
Q
is injective, let
m\in M
be any element such that
m\neq 0
. Then, the component of degree
r-1
of
Q(m)
is
x^{r-1}\otimes m
, which is different from
0
; we conclude that
Q(m)\neq 0
.

We are left to prove that
\ker(\Psi)\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(Q)
. Let
z=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes m_{i}
be any element in
S\otimes_{R}M
such that
\Psi(z)=0
or, equivalently,

\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i+1}\otimes m_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes x\cdot m_{i}.

Recalling that
x^{r}=-\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}a_{r-i}x^{i}
, by
R
-linearity we obtain

(1.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes(m_{i-1}-a_{r-i}\cdot m_{r-1})-a_{r}\otimes m_{r-1}=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes x\cdot m_{i}.

Let
m:=m_{r-1}
. Looking at the components in degree
r-1
of Equation
1.1
, we obtain

-a_{1}\cdot m+m_{r-2}=x\cdot m,

or, explicitely,

m_{r-2}=(x+a_{1})\cdot m=\sum_{j=0}^{1}(x^{1-j}a_{j})\cdot m

Proceeding by induction on
i
decreasing from
r-2
to
1
, suppose that the equality

m_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m

is proven. Then, looking at the components in degree
i
of Equation
1.1
, we obtain:

m_{i-1}-a_{r-i}\cdot m=x\cdot m_{i},

hence:

\displaystyle m_{i-1}

\displaystyle=x\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m+a_{r-i}\cdot m=

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{r-i}(x^{r-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m.

Hence, we conclude that
m_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m
for any
i
from
0
to
r-1
; in other terms:

z=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}(x^{r-1-i-j}a_{j})\cdot m\right)=Q(m)\in\operatorname{Im}(Q).

Finally, note that the map
Q=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{i}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1-i}\overline{x}^{r-1-i-j}\overline{a}_{j}
has been defined at the level of
S
-modules, so it defines only a local map of sheaves on
V
. In order to define a global map of sheaves, note that any power of
x
and
\overline{x}
, considered as global maps, introduces a twist by
\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L
up to the same tensor power, and any
\overline{a}_{j}
introduces a twist by
\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(L^{\otimes j})
. The sum of these twistings in the definition of
Q
, for any
i
and
j
in the sum, is equal to
i+(r-1-i-j)+j=r-1
. Hence, in order to extend the map
Q
globally, one has to consider a map

\mathcal{Q}:\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(L^{1-r})\rightarrow\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E

whose local expression equals the one of
Q
on any affine open
V=\pi_{\underline{a}}^{-1}(U)
such that
L
is trivial on
U
. Then, such map fits the global exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(L^{1-r})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E\xrightarrow{\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}(\Phi)-x}\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}E\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L\xrightarrow{\ev}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}L\rightarrow 0.

∎

Remark 1.4.6
.

Proposition
1.4.5
suggests that the spectral correspondence stated in
[
HP12
, Proposition 6.1]
is somehow misstated. Indeed, by definition of the spectral curve, the morphism
\pi_{\underline{a}}^{*}\Phi-x
has non-trivial kernel at each point of
X_{\underline{a}}
. In particular, any spectral data
\mathcal{M}
fitting in the exact sequence above needs to be supported on the whole
X_{\underline{a}}
. This is in general not true for torsion-free sheaves of polarized rank equal to 1, even for connected planar curves, as showed in
[
Lop04
, §2]
.

Chapter 2
The Norm map on the compactified Jacobian

In this chapter we study how to generalize the Norm map for line bundles to the compactified Jacobian stack. We look first at the geometric counterpart of a smaller problem, regarding the direct image of Cartier divisors and generalized divisors, in order to understand how to proceed in the general situation of torsion-free sheaves with rank 1.

Let
X\xrightarrow{\pi}Y
be any finite, flat morphism between embeddable noetherian schemes of pure dimension 1. In the first part of this chapter (Sections
2.1
,
2.2
), we review the classical Norm map and the theory of generalized divisors on curves, in relation with the objects introduced in Chapter
1
. In the second part (Section
2.3
) we propose the definitions of the direct and inverse image for generalized divisors and generalized line bundles, and we study their properties. In the third part of the chapter (Sections
2.4
and
2.5
), restricting to the case when
X
and
Y
are projective curves over a field, we consider the same notions for families. On one hand, families of effective generalized divisors on a curve
X
are essentially families of subschemes of finite length, hence they are parametrized by the Hilbert scheme. On the other hand, families of generalized line bundles on
X
are parametrized by the generalized Jacobian
\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
, and in this case the direct image map is called
Norm map
in analogy with the Norm map defined between the Jacobians of the curves. In both cases, we show that giving the definitions of the direct image and the Norm map on such moduli spaces is possible only when the curve
Y
is smooth. Finally, we show that the Norm map can be extended under the same hypothesis to the compactified Jacobian
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
.

Notation
. In the rest of the chapter, in the absence of further specifications, by
curve
we refer to a noetherian scheme of pure dimension 1 which is embeddable (i.e. it can be embedded as a closed subscheme of a regular scheme). This implies that the canonical (or dualizing) sheaf
\omega
of the curve is well defined.

2.1.
Review of the Norm map

We resume now the definition and properties of the direct and inverse image for Cartier divisors and the Norm map for line bundles, associated to a finite, flat morphism between curves. For a complete treatment, the standard reference is
[
Gro67
, §21]
together with
[
Gro61
, §6.5]
. We start with the definition of the norm at the level of sheaves of algebras.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism between curves of degree
n
. Since
Y
is noetherian, this is equivalent to require that
f
is finite and locally free, i.e. that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-algebra
[
Sta19
,
\href
https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/02K9Tag 02K9]
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

The sheaf
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is endowed with a homomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules, called the norm and defined on local sections by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}:\hskip 20.00003pt\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\longmapsto\det(\cdot s)

where
\cdot s:\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the multiplication map induced by
s
and
\det(\cdot s)
is given locally by the determinant of the matrix with entries in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
associated to
\cdot s
.

By the standard properties of determinants, for local sections
s,s^{\prime}
of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
and any section
\mu
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, we have:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(s\cdot s^{\prime})=\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(s)\cdot\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(s^{\prime}),\hskip 30.00005pt\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(\mu s)=\mu^{n}\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(s).

Before giving the definitions of the present section, we need a technical lemma.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of degree
n
between curves and let
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Then,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}
is an invertible
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module and there exists an open affine cover
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
Y
s.t.
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is trivial on each
V_{i}
and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}
is trivial both as a
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module and as an
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module on each
V_{i}
.

Proof.

By
[
Sta19
,
\href
https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/02K9Tag 02K9]
,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module; denote with
\{W_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
an open affine cover such that
{\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{|W_{\alpha}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{n}}_{|W_{\alpha}}
for each
\alpha\in A
. By
[
Gro61
, Proposition 6.1.12]
,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}
is an invertible
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module; denote with
\{W^{\prime}_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in B}
an open affine cover such that
{\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}}_{|W^{\prime}_{\beta}}\simeq{\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{|W^{\prime}_{\beta}}
for each
\beta\in B
. Let
\{V_{\alpha,\beta}=W_{\alpha}\cap W^{\prime}_{\beta}\}_{(\alpha,\beta)\in A\times B}
be a common refinement. Then, for
I=A\times B
,
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an open affine cover of
Y
such that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is trivial on each open of the cover and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}
is trivial both as
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module and as
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module on each open.
∎

2.1.1.
Direct and inverse image of Cartier divisors

We recall now the definitions of direct and inverse image for Cartier divisors. For any curve
X
, denote with
\mathcal{K}_{X}
the sheaf of total quotient rings of the curve. Recall that the set of
Cartier divisors
on
X
is the set of global sections of the quotient sheaf of multiplicative groups
\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}
:

\operatorname{CDiv}(X)=\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}).

Although the group operation on
\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}
is multiplication, the group operation on
\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
is denoted additively. The group of Cartier divisors of
X
contains the subgroup
\operatorname{Prin}(X)
of
principal divisors
defined as the image of the canonical homomorphism

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*})\longrightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}).

It is well known
[
Gro67
, §21.2]
that the set of Cartier divisors is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of invertible fractional ideals, i.e. the set of subsheaves
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{X}
that are also invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
[
Gro67
, Proposition 21.2.6]
. If
D\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})
is represented by an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and a collection of sections
f_{i}\in\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*})
such that
f_{i}/f_{j}\in\Gamma(U_{i}\cap U_{j},\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})
, the corresponding fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{D}
is the sub
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of
\mathcal{K}_{X}
equal to
\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}\cdot f_{i}
on any
U_{i}
.
1
1

1

Differently from Grothendieck’s EGA, we pick
f_{i}
instead of
f_{i}^{-1}
. This does not affect the other results.
Under this correspondence, the sum of Cartier divisors corresponds to the multiplication of fractional ideals.

Definition/Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism between curves and let
\pi^{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
be the associated canonical map of sheaves of modules.

Let
D\in\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
be a Cartier divisor on
X
corresponding to the invertible fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}
and let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an affine cover as in Lemma
2.1.2
. Then, on each
V_{i}
,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|V_{i}}
is equal to the subsheaf
h_{i}\cdot(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}
of
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{K}_{X})_{|V_{i}}
generated by a meromorphic regular section
h_{i}=f_{i}/g_{i}
, with
f_{i},g_{i}\in\Gamma(V_{i},\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})
. The
direct image of
D
, denoted
\pi_{*}(D)
, is the Cartier divisor on
Y
corresponding to the fractional ideal generated on any
V_{i}
by the meromorphic regular section
\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(f_{i})/\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(g_{i})\in\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{K}_{Y})
.

Let
M\in\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)
be a Cartier divisor on
Y
corresponding to the invertible fractional ideal
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{Y}
, and let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an affine cover of
Y
such that, on each
V_{i}
,
\mathcal{J}_{|V_{i}}
is equal to the fractional ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{Y|V_{i}}
-modules generated by a meromorphic regular section
u_{i}=s_{i}/t_{i}
with
s_{i},t_{i}\in\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{*})
. The
inverse image
of
M
, denoted
\pi^{*}(M)
, is the Cartier divisor on
X
corresponding to the fractional ideal generated on any
U_{i}=\pi^{-1}(V_{i})
by the meromorphic regular section
\pi^{\sharp}(s_{i})/\pi^{\sharp}(t_{i})\in\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{K}_{X})
.

Proof.

The definition of direct image is a reformulation of the one given in
[
Gro67
, §21.5.5]
. The fact that
\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(f_{i})/\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(g_{i})
is a regular meromorphic section follows from the discussion in
[
Gro67
, §21.5.3]
; the definition is independent of the choice of the
h_{i}
’s since the norm of sheaves is multiplicative.

The definition of inverse image is a reformulation of
[
Gro67
, Definition 21.4.2]
, obtained as a consequence of
[
Gro67
, §21.4.3]
, together with the result of
[
Gro67
, Proposition 21.4.5]
that ensures that
\pi^{\sharp}(s_{i})/\pi^{\sharp}(t_{i})
is regular.
∎

Proposition 2.1.4
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of curves of degree
n
. The direct and inverse image for Cartier divisors induce homorphisms of groups:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}:\operatorname{CDiv}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}:\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{CDiv}(X),

such that
\pi_{*}\circ\pi^{*}
is the multiplication map by
n
.

Proof.

The direct image is a homomorphism thanks to
[
Gro67
, §21.5.5.1]
, while the inverse image is a homomorphism as a consequence of
[
Gro67
, Definition 21.5.5.1]
. The result on
\pi_{*}\circ\pi^{*}
is stated in
[
Gro67
, Proposition 21.5.6]

∎

2.1.2.
Norm of line bundles

We come now to the definition of the Norm map for invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.

Definition/Lemma 2.1.5
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism between curves and let
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an affine cover of
Y
as in Lemma
2.1.2
. In particular, there is for any
i\in I
an isomorphism
\lambda_{i}:(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})_{|V_{i}}\rightarrow(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}
. For any
i,j\in I
, the isomorphism
\omega_{ij}:=\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}^{-1}
can be
interpreted as a section of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
over
V_{i}\cap V_{j}
. The collection of norms
\{\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(\omega_{ij})\}_{i,j\in I}
is a 1-cocycle with values in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{*}
.

The cocyle
\{\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(\omega_{ij})\}_{i,j\in I}
defines up to isomorphism an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module, which is called the
Norm of
\mathcal{L}

relative to

\pi
and is denoted as
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})
or
\operatorname{Nm}_{Y/X}(\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}
through an isomorphism
h:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
, then a local trivialization for
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
over
V_{i}
is given by
\lambda_{i}\circ(\pi_{*}h)
. Running over all
i\in I
, the resulting 1-cocycle
\{\mathcal{N}_{Y/X}(\lambda_{i}\circ\pi_{*}h\circ\pi_{*}h^{-1}\circ\lambda_{j}^{-1})\}_{i,j\in I}
is the same as for
\mathcal{L}
.
∎

Recall that, for any curve
X
, the Picard group of
X
is the set
\operatorname{Pic}(X)
of isomorphism classes of invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, endowed with the operation of tensor product.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of curves of degree
n
. The Norm and the inverse image map for line bundles induce homomorphism of groups:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}:\operatorname{Pic}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(Y)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}:\operatorname{Pic}(Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pic}(X),

such that
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}\circ\pi^{*}
is the
n
-th tensor power.

Proof.

The inverse image for line bundles is a homomorphism since it commutes obviously with tensor products. The other results follow from (
2.1
), using the fact that tensor product of line bundles corresponds to multiplication at the level of defining cocycles.
∎

Proposition 2.1.7
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism between curves. For any invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{L}
, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

Proof.

See
[
HP12
, Corollary 3.12]
.
∎

As we show in the next proposition, the direct image for Cartier divisors and the Norm map for line bundles are related, as well as the inverse image maps. Recall that, on any curve
X
, the Picard group is canonically isomorphic to the group of Cartier divisors modulo the subgroup
\operatorname{Prin}(X)
of principal divisors. This gives rise to a canonical quotient of groups:

\displaystyle q_{X}:\operatorname{CDiv}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{CDiv}(X)/\operatorname{Prin}(X)=\operatorname{Pic}(X)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{I}_{D}.

Proposition 2.1.8
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism between curves. The direct and inverse image for Cartier divisors are compatible via the quotient maps
q_{X}
and
q_{Y}
respectively with the Norm and the inverse image map for line bundles; i.e. the following diagrams of groups are commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}

Proof.

The commutativity of the first diagram follows from correspondence between
D
and
\mathcal{I}_{D}
and the definitions of
\pi_{*}
and
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}
. The second diagram is commutative as consequence of
[
Gro67
, Picture 21.4.2.1]
together with
[
Gro67
, Proposition 21.4.5]
.
∎

We are finally interested in reviewing the Norm map for families of line bundles.
2
2

2

For the sake of completeness, we note that a complete discussion regarding families of Cartier divisors and their direct and inverse images is done in
[
Gro67
, §21.15]
.
To study families of line bundles, we
assume that
X
and
Y
are projective curves over a base field
k
and that
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a finite, flat morphism of degree
n
defined over
k
. Families of line bundles are parametrized by the Jacobian schemes of
X
and
Y
(denoted
J(X)
and
J(Y)
, see Chapter
1
, Section
1.2
).

Definition/Lemma 2.1.9
.

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of projective curves over a base field
k
. For any
k
-scheme
T
, the
Norm map for line bundles associated to
\pi
is defined on the
T
-valued points of the Jacobian of
X
as:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(T):\hskip 20.00003ptJ(X)(T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J(Y)(T)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}.

where
\pi_{T}:X\times_{k}T\rightarrow Y\times_{k}T
is induced by pullback from
\pi
.

Proof.

We need to check that the map is well defined. Since
\mathcal{L}
is a line bundle on
X\times_{k}T
, its pushforward
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})
is a locally free sheaf of rank
n
on
Y\times_{k}T
, as for
\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}
. Taking determinants produces line bundles, so
\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}
is a well-defined line bundle on
Y\times_{k}T
. The whole construction is functorial, so the above definition gives a well-defined morphism of schemes

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}:\hskip 20.00003ptJ(X)\longrightarrow J(Y).

∎

2.2.
Review of generalized divisors

In this section, we review known facts about generalized divisors and generalized line bundles on curves. Moreover, we compare their moduli spaces with the compactified Jacobian.

Remark 2.2.1
.

The theory of generalized divisors is developed by Hartshorne in his papers
[
Har86
]
,
[
Har94
]
and
[
Har07
]
, in order to generalize the notion of Cartier divisor on schemes satisfying condition
S_{2}
of Serre. Since we are dealing with schemes of dimension 1, the condition
S_{2}
of Serre coincides with the condition
S_{1}
, which in turn coincides with the fact of not having embedded components (i.e. embedded points). In particular, any scheme of pure dimension 1 is also
S_{1}
, and hence
S_{2}
. Similarly, a coherent sheaf on a curve satisfies condition
S_{2}
if and only if it is torsion-free.

2.2.1.
Generalized divisors

Definition 2.2.2
.

Let
X
be a curve and let
\mathcal{K}_{X}
be the sheaf of total quotient rings on
X
. A
generalized divisor
on
X
is a nondegenerate fractional ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, i.e. a subsheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{X}
that is a coherent sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules and such that
\mathcal{I}_{\eta}=\mathcal{K}_{X,\eta}
for any generic point
\eta\in X
. It is
effective
if
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X}
. It is
Cartier
if
\mathcal{I}
is an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module, or equivalently locally principal. It is
principal
if
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\cdot f
(also denoted
(f)
) for some global section
f\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}^{*}_{X})
.

The set of generalized divisors on
X
is denoted with
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
, the subset of Cartier divisors with
\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
and the set of principal divisors with
\operatorname{Prin}(X)
.

Using the usual notion of subscheme associated to a sheaf of ideals, the set
\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
of effective generalized divisors on
X
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of closed subschemes
D\subset X
of pure codimension one (i.e. of dimension zero). With a slight abuse of notation, also for non-effective divisors, we denote with
D
the generalized divisor and we refer to
\mathcal{I}
(or
\mathcal{I}_{D}
) as the
fractional ideal
of
D
(also called
defining ideal
of
D
, or
ideal sheaf
of
D
if
D
is effective).

Let
D_{1},D_{2}
be generalized divisors on
X
, with fractional ideals
\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}
. The
sum

D_{1}+D_{2}
is the generalized divisor associated to the fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{X}
. The sum is commutative, associative, with neutral element
0
defined by the trivial ideal
\mathcal{O}_{X}
. The
inverse
of a generalized divisor
D
associated to
\mathcal{I}
is the generalized divisor
-D
associated to the fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}^{-1}
, i.e. the sheaf which on any open subset
U
of
X
is defined as
\{f\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{K}_{X})\hskip 3.00003pt|\hskip 3.00003pt\mathcal{I}(U)\cdot f\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)\}
.

The inverse operation behaves well with the sum only for Cartier divisors. For any pair of generalized divisors
D,E
on
X
with
E
Cartier,
-(D+E)=(-D)+(-E)
, but
D+(-D)=0
if and only if
D
is a Cartier divisor. As a consequence, the set
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
of all generalized divisors over
X
endowed with the sum operation is not a group, but the subset
\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
of Cartier divisors is. The set
\operatorname{Prin}(X)
of principal divisors is a subgroup of
\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
and both the groups act by addition on the set
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
.

Tthe following Lemma, inspired by
[
Har94
, Proposition 2.11]
, shows that any generalized divisor is equal to the sum of an effective generalized divisor and the inverse of an effective Cartier divisor, as

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Let
X
be a curve and let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be any generalized divisor on
X
. Then, there exist an effective generalized divisor
D^{\prime}
and an effective Cartier divisor
E
on
X
such that
D=D^{\prime}+(-E)
.

Proof.

Cover
X
by open affines
U_{i}=\operatorname{Spec}(A_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,r
. For each
i
, denote with
I_{i}
the fractional ideal of
D
restricted to
U_{i}
. This is a finitely generated
A_{i}
-module, so there exists a nonzero-divisor
f_{i}\in A_{i}
such that
f\cdot I_{i}\subseteq A
. Let
Y_{i}\subset U_{i}
be the closed subscheme defined by
f_{i}
, which is an effective Cartier divisor of
U_{i}
; after the composition with
U_{i}\hookrightarrow X
,
Y_{i}
becomes an effective Cartier divisor of
X
. Now, the sum of divisors
D+\sum_{i=1}^{r}Y_{i}
is effective as it is sum of effective divisors on each
U_{i}
. By putting
E=\sum_{i=1}^{r}Y_{i}
and
D^{\prime}=D+E
we get the result.
∎

Two generalized divisors
D_{1},D_{2}
over
X
are
linearly equivalent
(written
D_{1}\sim D_{2}
) if there is a divisor
(f)\in\operatorname{Prin}(X)
such that
D_{1}+(f)=D_{2}
. We define the
generalized Picard of X
as the set of divisors on
X
modulo linear equivalence:

\operatorname{GPic}(X)=\operatorname{GDiv}(X)/\operatorname{Prin}(X).

We have the following commutative diagram of sets, where the vertical maps are quotients by
\operatorname{Prin}(X)
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{CDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pic}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{GPic}(X)}

Taking inverse and sums preserve linear equivalence, so the two operations are well defined also on
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
and the subset
\operatorname{Pic}(X)\subseteq\operatorname{GPic}(X)
is a group that acts on
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
by addition. For a curve
X
, the condition
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)=\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
is also equivalent to
\operatorname{GPic}(X)=\operatorname{Pic}(X)
, which is also equivalent to the curve
X
being smooth.

The set
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
has an alternative description in terms of generalized line bundles. Let
D
be a generalized divisor on
X
; then, its fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}
is a generalized line bundle. If
D^{\prime}
is another generalized line bundle, it is linearly equivalent to
D
if and only if its fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a generalized line bundle isomorphic to
X
as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Viceversa, any generalized line bundle
\mathcal{F}
on
X
is isomorphic to the fractional ideal of some generalized divisor
D

[
Har07
, Proposition 2.4]
. Then,
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
can be also defined as the set of generalized line bundles of
X
, up to isomorphism
.
Under this description, classes of Cartier divisors correspond to isomorphism classes of line bundles, and the operations of sum by a Cartier divisor and inverse of a divisor are replaced with tensor product and taking dual of the corresponding sheaves.

2.2.2.
Degree of generalized divisors

We now
assume that
X
is a projective curve over a base field
k
. In this case, the notion of
degree of a divisor
can be introduced.

Definition/Lemma 2.2.4
.

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective generalized divisor on
X
with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and let
x\in X
be any point of
X
in codimension 1. We define the
degree of
D
at
x
as the non-negative integer:

\deg_{x}(D)=\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{X,x}}\big(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathcal{I}_{D,x}\big)[\kappa(x):k].

The degree of any generalized divisor
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
at
x
is defined as
\deg_{x}(D)=\deg_{x}(E)-\deg_{x}(F)
, where
D=E-F
with
E,F
effective generalized divisors and
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
.

The degree of
D
on
X
(also denoted as
\deg(D)
when there is no ambiguity) is equal to the sum of the degrees of
D
at all points of
X
in codimension 1:

\deg_{X}(D)=\sum_{x\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{x}(D).

Proof.

First, note that the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathcal{I}_{D,x}
in nonzero if and only if
x
is in the support of
D
. In such case the local ring has Krull dimension 0, so it is Artinian and hence has finite length.

The definition of degree at a point of any generalized divisor is well-given thanks to
[
Har94
, Lemma 2.17]
, and
\deg_{x}(-D)=-\deg_{x}(D)
.

Finally, the supporty of
D
contains only finitely many points, hence the degree of
D
on
X
is a well-defined integer.
∎

The degree of a generalized divisor is strictly related to the degree of its fractional ideal, as the following proposition shows.

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Let
D
be a generalized divisor on
X
with fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{X}
. Then, the degree of
\mathcal{I}_{D}
as a torsion-free sheaf is well defined and equal to
-\deg_{X}(D)
. Moreover, linearly equivalent divisors have the same degree on
X
.

Proof.

The fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{D}
is a generalized line bundle, hence it has rank
1
on
X
and well-defined degree as a torsion-free sheaf. In order to compute
\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})
consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D}\rightarrow 0.

By definition of degree,
\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})=\chi(\mathcal{I}_{D})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Then, by additivity of Euler characteristic with respect to exact sequences, we deduce that
\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})=-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D})
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D}
has dimension 0, its Euler characteristic is equal to
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D}):=\dim_{k}H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D})
. The support of
\mathcal{O}_{D}=\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D}
is by definition the support of
D
and
H^{0}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D})=\oplus_{x\in\operatorname{Supp}D}\mathcal{O}_{D,x}
. Then we compute:

\displaystyle\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})

\displaystyle=-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}_{D})=-\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Supp}D}\dim_{k}\mathcal{O}_{D,x}=

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Supp}D}\dim_{\kappa(x)}\big(\mathcal{O}_{D,x}\big)[\kappa(x):k]=

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Supp}D}\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{X},x}\big(\mathcal{O}_{D,x}\big)[\kappa(x):k]=

\displaystyle=-\deg_{X}(D).

Consider now a generalized divisor
D
with fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{D}
and let
D=E-F
with
E,F
effective and
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
. Since
F
is effective, consider first the short exact sequence:

(2.3)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow T\rightarrow 0

where
T
is a sheaf of dimension 0. Since
F
is Cartier, we can tensor by
\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}
to obtain:

(2.4)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\otimes T\rightarrow 0.

Using addivity of Euler characteristics in the exact sequences
2.3
and
2.4
and the fact that
\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\otimes T)=\chi(T)
, we see that:

\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})-\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}).

Also
E
is effective, hence there is a short exact sequence:

(2.5)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow Q\rightarrow 0

where
Q
is a sheaf of dimension 0. Tensoring again by
\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}
we obtain:

(2.6)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\otimes Q\rightarrow 0

where
\mathcal{I}_{D}\simeq\mathcal{I}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}
since
D=E-F
and
F
is Cartier. Using addivity of Euler characteristics in the exact sequences
2.5
and
2.6
and the fact that
\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\otimes Q)=\chi(Q)
, we can compute the degree of
\mathcal{I}_{D}
:

\displaystyle\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{I}_{D})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})=

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1})-\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1}\otimes Q)-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})=

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1})-\chi(Q)-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})=

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F}^{-1})-2\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})+\chi(\mathcal{I}_{E})=

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})-\chi(\mathcal{I}_{F})+\chi(\mathcal{I}_{E})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Since
E
and
F
are effective, we conclude that:

\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})=-(\deg_{X}(E)-\deg_{X}(F))=-\deg_{X}(D).

To prove the last statement, let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be linearly equivalent divisors; then, their fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}_{D}
and
\mathcal{I}_{D^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. Hence,

\deg_{X}(D)=-\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D})=-\deg(\mathcal{I}_{D}^{\prime})=\deg_{X}(D^{\prime}).

∎

2.2.3.
Moduli of generalized divisors and the Abel Map

We start with considering the moduli space for generalized divisors. Since we are dealing with families of sheaves and related moduli problems, we
assume that
X
is a projective curve over a base field
k
.

Remark 2.2.6
.

In general
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
cannot be represented by a geometric object of finite type, even for fixed degrees. Also, it is not easy to give a correct definition for flat families of non-effective generalized divisors. Hence, only families of effective divisors are considered, using the Hilbert scheme.

Definition 2.2.7
.

The
Hilbert scheme of effective generalized divisors of degree
d
on
X
is the Hilbert scheme
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}
parametrizing 0-dimensional subschemes of
X
, with Hilbert polynomial equal to a costant integer
d
. Recall that, given a
k
-scheme
T
, a
T
-valued point of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}
is a
T
-flat subscheme
D\subset X\times_{k}T
such that
D
restricted to the fiber over any
t\in T
is a 0-dimensional subscheme of
X\times_{k}\{t\}\simeq X
, of degree
d
. In other words, the corresponding ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}_{D}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}
restricted to any fiber over
T
defines a generalized divisor
D_{t}
on
X
.

The
Hilbert scheme of effective Cartier divisors of degree
d
is the open subscheme
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{d}\subseteq\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}
parametrizing subschemes of
X
of degree
d
whose ideal sheaf is locally principal.

Note that
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{d}=\operatorname{Hilb}^{d}_{X}
when
X
is smooth.

Families of effective generalized divisors can be added with families of Cartier divisors, giving rise to a morphism:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}\times\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{e}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d+e}

\displaystyle(D,E)\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{I}_{D}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{E}).

Effective generalized divisors on
X
up to linear equivalence are generalized line bundles, hence they are parametrized by the generalized Jacobian
\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
.

By Remark
1.2.9
, the Jacobian scheme
J(X)
of
X
is contained in
\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
as an open subscheme. The operations of tensor products and taking inverse are well defined on
J(X)
and make
J(X)
into an algebraic group, acting on
\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
via tensor product:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)\times J(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{E})\hskip 15.00002pt

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{E}.

Finally, we recall the definition of the geometric Abel map. Let
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}\operatorname{Hilb}^{d}_{X}
be the Hilbert scheme of effective generalized divisor of any degree on
X
.

Definition 2.2.8
.

Let
M
be a line bundle of degree
e
on
X
. The
(
M
-twisted) Abel map
of degree
d
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{M}^{d}:\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X,-d+e)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{I}_{D}\otimes M.

The
M
-twisted Abel map in any degree is defined similary as a map:

\mathcal{A}_{M}:\hskip 10.00002pt\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)

The restriction of
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{d}
to
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{d}
takes values in
J(X,-d+e)
. Taking care of the twisting, the Abel map is equivariant with respect to the sum of effective Cartier divisors: for any pair of line bundles
M
and
N
on
X
and for any
D\in\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
,
E\in\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
,

\mathcal{A}_{M\otimes N}(D+E)\simeq\mathcal{A}_{M}(D)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{N}(E).

2.3.
The direct and inverse image for generalized divisors and generalized line bundles

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of degree
n
between curves. In the present section, we extend the notion of direct and inverse image from Cartier divisors to generalized divisors and generalized line bundles.

2.3.1.
The Fitting ideal

A fundamental tool for the definition of the direct image for generalized divisors is the
Fitting ideal of a module
(and
of a sheaf of modules
), of which we recall here the definition. See
[
Eis95
, Chapter 20]
,
[
Vas04
, Chapter 2.4]
and
[
EH06
, Chapter V.1.3]
for a detailed treatment.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
X
be a scheme and let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on
X
. Let

\mathcal{E}_{1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\psi\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{E}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\rightarrow 0

be any finite presentation of
\mathcal{F}
, with
\mathcal{E}_{0}
locally free of rank
r
. The
0
-th Fitting ideal
of
\mathcal{F}
, denoted
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\mathcal{F})
, is defined as the image of the map:

\textstyle{\bigwedge^{r}}\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{r}}\mathcal{E}_{0}\right)^{-1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\Psi\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{O}_{X}

induced by
\bigwedge^{r}\psi:\bigwedge^{r}\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow\bigwedge^{r}\mathcal{E}_{0}
and is independent of the choiche of the presentation for
\mathcal{F}
. If
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is locally free, then
\psi
can be locally represented by a matrix and the
0
-th Fitting ideal is generated locally by the minors of size
r
of such matrix, with the convention that the determinant of the
0\times 0
matrix is
1
.

Proof.

The definition is the sheaf-theoretic reformulation of
[
Eis95
, Corollary-Definition 20.4]
.
∎

As a consequence of the definition, the formation of Fitting ideals commutes with restrictions and with base change
[
Eis95
, Corollary 20.5]
.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on
X
. The
0
-th Fitting scheme
of
\mathcal{F}
is the subscheme of
X
defined as the zero locus of
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X}
in
X
. The
0
-th Fitting scheme contains the support of
\mathcal{F}
, as a closed subscheme with the same underlying topological space.

Proof.

The result is stated in
[
EH06
, Definition V-10]
.
∎

2.3.2.
The direct image

Here, we define the notion of direct image for generalized divisors. First, we start with the case of an effective generalized divisor. Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective generalized divisor on
X
, with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
. Since
\pi
is finite and flat, the pushforward
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.3
.

(Direct image of an effective generalized divisor)
Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective generalized divisor on
X
, with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X}
. The
direct image of D with respect to

\pi
, denoted with
\pi_{*}(D)
, is the effective generalized divisor on
Y
defined by the
0
-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
.

Proof.

Let us prove that the 0-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
defines an effective generalized divisor on
Y
. First note that
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
is a subsheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, and is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module; hence, it is an effective fractional ideal. To prove that
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
is nondegenerate, consider a generic point
\eta\in Y
. Since the map
\pi
is dominant, the preimage
\pi^{-1}(\eta)=\{\eta_{i}\}
is a finite set of generic points of
X
. Then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}))_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})_{\eta})=

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}((0)_{\eta})=\mathcal{O}_{X,\eta}=\mathcal{K}_{X,\eta}.

∎

Lemma 2.3.4
.

(Linearity w.r.t effective Cartier divisors)

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective generalized divisor and
E\in\operatorname{CDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective Cartier divisor on
X
. Then,
\pi_{*}(E)
is a Cartier divisor on
Y
and
\pi_{*}(D+E)=\pi_{*}(D)+\pi_{*}(E)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be the ideal sheaf of
E
and let
\{V_{i}\}
be an open affine cover of
Y
such that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}
are trivial on each
V_{i}
as in Lemma
2.1.2
. The sheaf
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}
is locally presented by the exact sequence:

(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}\xrightarrow{\cdot h_{i}}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}\rightarrow(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J})_{|V_{i}}\rightarrow 0

where
h_{i}\in\Gamma(\pi^{-1}(V_{i}),\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a local generator for
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
on
\pi^{-1}(V_{i})
. Since
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}
is a free
(\mathcal{O}_{Y})_{|V_{i}}
-module of rank
n
, there is a
n\times n
matrix
H_{i}
with entries in
(\mathcal{O}_{Y})_{|V_{i}}
representing the multiplication by
h_{i}
. Then, by Definition
2.3.1
, the 0-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}
is the principal ideal generated locally by
\det(\cdot h_{i})
on
V_{i}
. In particular,
\pi_{*}(E)
is a Cartier divisor.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the ideal sheaf of
D
, so that
\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J}
is the ideal sheaf of
D+E
. To prove the remaining part of the thesis, we show that the equality

\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J})=\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})\cdot\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J})

holds locally around any point
y\in Y
. Let
V
be an open neighborhood of
y
such that
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-module,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|V}
is generated by sections
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}
of
\Gamma(V,\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}_{|V}
is a principal ideal generated by a section
h
of
\Gamma(V,\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
. In terms of exact sequences:

\displaystyle(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}^{\oplus r}\xrightarrow{(\cdot s_{1},\dots,\cdot s_{r})}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}\rightarrow(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})_{|V}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}\xrightarrow{\cdot h}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}\rightarrow(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J})_{|V}\rightarrow 0

The ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J}
is equal to
\mathcal{J}\cdot\mathcal{I}
, which is generated on
V
by the sections
hs_{1},\dots,hs_{n}
. In terms of exact sequences:

\displaystyle(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}^{\oplus r}\xrightarrow{(\cdot hs_{1},\dots,\cdot hs_{r})}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}\rightarrow(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J})_{|V}\rightarrow 0

Denote with
S_{i}
and
H
the
(\mathcal{O}_{Y})_{|V}
-matrices representing the multiplication by
s_{i}
and
h
respectively. The map
(\cdot hs_{1},\dots,\cdot hs_{r})
in the previous exact sequence is represented by the
n\times nr
matrix

M=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}HS_{1}&\dots&HS_{r}\end{array}\right]=H\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}S_{1}&\dots&S_{r}\end{array}\right]

The 0-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J}
, restricted to
V
, is the ideal of
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}
generated by the
n\times n
minors of the matrix
M
. Any such minor is equal to a
n\times n
minor of the matrix
\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}S_{1}&\dots&S_{r}\end{array}\right]
multiplied by
\det H
. Then, by Definition
2.3.1
,

\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J})_{|V}=\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})_{|V}\cdot\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J})_{|V}

∎

Definition/Lemma 2.3.5
.

(Direct image of a generalized divisor)
Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be a generalized divisor on
X
, such that
D=D^{\prime}-E
with
D^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
and
E\in\operatorname{CDiv}^{+}(X)
by Lemma
2.2.3
. The
direct image of D with respect to
\pi
, denoted with
\pi_{*}(D)
, is the generalized divisor
\pi_{*}(D^{\prime})-\pi_{*}(E)
.

Proof.

To prove that it is well defined, let
D=D^{\prime}-E=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}-\widetilde{E}
, with
D^{\prime},\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
effective and
E,\widetilde{E}
effective Cartier.
Since
E,\widetilde{E}
are Cartier,
D^{\prime}+\widetilde{E}=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}+E
; then, by Lemma
2.3.4
we have:

\pi_{*}(D^{\prime})+\pi_{*}(\widetilde{E})=\pi_{*}(\widetilde{D}^{\prime})+\pi_{*}(E).

Since
\pi_{*}(E)
and
\pi_{*}(\widetilde{E})
are also Cartier by Lemma
2.3.4
, they can be subtracted from each side in order to obtain:

\pi_{*}(D^{\prime})-\pi_{*}(E)=\pi_{*}(\widetilde{D}^{\prime})-\pi_{*}(\widetilde{E}).

This shows that
\pi_{*}(D)
does not depend on the choice of
D^{\prime}
and
E
.
∎

We study now some properties of the direct image for generalized divisors.

Proposition 2.3.6
.

(Properties of direct image)

(1)

Let
D\in\operatorname{CDiv}(X)
be a Cartier divisor. Then,
\pi_{*}(D)
is a Cartier divisor and
\pi_{*}(-D)=-\pi_{*}(D)
. Moreover,
\pi_{*}(D)
coincides with
\pi_{*}(D)
of Definition
2.1.3
.

(2)

Let
D,E\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be generalized divisors, such that
E
is Cartier. Then,
\pi_{*}(D+E)=\pi_{*}(D)+\pi_{*}(E)
.

(3)

Let
V\subset Y
be an open subset, and denote with
\pi_{U}
the restriction of
\pi
to
U=\pi^{-1}(V)\subset X
. Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be a generalized divisor on
X
. Then,

(\pi_{U})_{*}(D_{|U})=\pi_{*}(D)_{|V}

(4)

Let
D,D^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be generalized divisors such that
D\sim D^{\prime}
. Then,
\pi_{*}(D)\sim\pi_{*}(D^{\prime})
.

Proof.

To prove (1), consider
D=E-F
with
E,F
effective and
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
. Since
D
is Cartier and
E=D+F
, then also
E
is Cartier. By Definition
2.3.5
,

\pi_{*}(D)=\pi_{*}(E)-\pi_{*}(F).

By Lemma
2.3.4
, it is a difference of Cartier divisors and hence it is Cartier. To compute
\pi_{*}(-D)
, note that since
F
is Cartier then
-D=F-E
, and it is a difference of effective Cartier divisors; then, apply Definition
2.3.5
to obtain

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(-D)

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(F)-\pi_{*}(E)=

\displaystyle=-\pi_{*}(D).

To compare
\pi_{*}(D)
with Definition
2.1.3
, let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the ideal sheaves of
E
and
F
and let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an open cover of
Y
such that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|V_{i}}
and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}_{|V_{i}}
are non-degenerate principal ideals of
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V_{i}}
-modules generated by the regular sections
f_{i}
and
g_{i}
of
\Gamma(V,\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
, respectively on each
i\in I
. The fractional ideal of
D
is then generated on each
V_{i}
by the meromorphic regular section
h_{i}=f_{i}/g_{i}
of
\Gamma(V_{i},\pi_{*}\mathcal{K}_{X}^{*})
.
By the proof of Lemma
2.3.4
, the ideal sheaves of
\pi_{*}(E)
and
\pi_{*}(F)
are generated on each
V_{i}
by
\det(\cdot f_{i})
and
\det(\cdot g_{i})
, so the fractional ideal of
\pi_{*}(D)
is generated on each
V_{i}
by the meromorphic regular section
\det(\cdot f_{i})/\det(\cdot g_{i})
of
\Gamma(V,\mathcal{K}_{Y}^{*})
. Using the same cover in Definition
2.1.3
and applying Definition
2.1.1
, the sheaf
\pi_{*}(D)
defined above and the sheaf
\pi_{*}(D)
defined in Section
2.1
have the same local generators, hence they are equal.

To prove (2), consider
D=D_{1}-D_{2}
and
E=E_{1}-E_{2}
, with
D_{1},D_{2},E_{1},E_{2}
effective and
D_{2},E_{2}
Cartier. Note that
D+E=(D_{1}+E_{1})-(D_{2}+E_{2})
, and it is a difference of effective divisors with
D_{2}+E_{2}
Cartier. Then, applying Definition
2.3.5
and Lemma
2.3.4
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(D+E)

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}((D_{1}+E_{1})-(D_{2}+E_{2}))=

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(D_{1}+E_{1})-\pi_{*}(D_{2}+E_{2})=

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(D_{1})+\pi_{*}(E_{1})-\pi_{*}(D_{2})-\pi_{*}(E_{2})=

\displaystyle=(\pi_{*}(D_{1})-\pi_{*}(D_{2}))-(\pi_{*}(E_{1})-\pi_{*}(E_{2}))=

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(D)+\pi_{*}(E).

To prove (3), if
D
is effective, the result follows from Definition
2.3.1
. Then, observe that the operations of product and inverse of fractional ideals commute with restrictions.

To prove (4), let
f\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{K}_{X})
be a global section that generates a principal divisor
E=(f)\in\operatorname{Prin}(X)
. By linearity, it is sufficient to prove that
\pi_{*}(E)
is again principal. Let
\{V_{i}\}
be an affine open cover of
Y
, such that locally

f_{|\pi^{-1}(V_{i})}=g_{i}/h_{i},\hskip 30.00005ptg_{i},h_{i}\in\Gamma(\pi^{-1}(V_{i}),\mathcal{O}_{X})

In terms of divisors, this means that
(f)_{|\pi^{-1}(V_{i})}=(g_{i})-(h_{i})
, and it is a difference of effective Cartier divisors on
\pi^{-1}(V_{i})
. Then, applying Part (3) and reasoning simiarly to Part (1), we obtain:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}((f))_{|V}

\displaystyle=(\pi_{i})_{*}((g_{i}))-(\pi_{i})_{*}((h_{i}))=

\displaystyle=(\det[\cdot g_{i}])-(\det[\cdot h_{i}])=

\displaystyle=(\det[\cdot g_{i}]/\det[\cdot h_{i}]).

Since taking determinants is multiplicative, the local sections
\det[\cdot g_{i}]/\det[\cdot h_{i}]
glue together to give a global section
\widetilde{f}
of
\mathcal{K}_{Y}
, such that
\pi_{*}(E)=(\widetilde{f})
.
∎

We are now ready to define the direct image for generalized line bundles.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.7
.

(Direct image for generalized line bundles)
The direct image for generalized divisors induce a direct image map between the sets of generalized line bundles, defined as:

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}]:\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{GPic}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{GPic}(Y)

\displaystyle[D]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\pi_{*}(D)].

Proof.

Recall that for any curve
X
, the set
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
can be seen equivalently as the set of generalized line bundles or as the set of generalized divisors modulo linear equivalence. Here,
[\pi_{*}]
is defined in terms of generalized divisors modulo linear equivalence. By Proposition
2.3.6
, the direct images of linearly equivalent divisors are linearly equivalent, hence
[\pi_{*}]
is well defined.
∎

In the remaining part of this subsection, we study an alternative formula for
[\pi_{*}]
in terms of generalized line bundles. First, we need a technical lemma.

Lemma 2.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on a curve
X
which is locally free of rank 1 at any generic point and let
\omega=\omega_{X}
be the canonical, or dualizing sheaf of
X
. Let
T(\mathcal{F})
be the torsion subsheaf of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}=\Hhom(\Hhom(\mathcal{F},\omega),\omega)
be the double
\omega
-dual. There, there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{F}^{tf}=\mathcal{F}/T(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}.

Proof.

The sheaf
\mathcal{F}^{tf}
is endowed with a quotient map
q:\mathcal{F}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{tf}
, which is universal among all arrows from
\mathcal{F}
to torsion-free sheaves (i.e. pure of dimension 1). Note that, since
\mathcal{F}
is locally free of rank 1 at any generic point of
X
, then
q
is generically an isomorphism. Let
\alpha(\mathcal{F}):\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}
be the canonical map from
\mathcal{F}
to its double
\omega
-dual. Since taking double
\omega
-duals is functorial, there is a commutative diagram of homomorphism of sheaves:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{tf}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{F}^{tf})^{\omega\omega}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(\mathcal{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(\mathcal{F}^{tf})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\omega\omega}}

Using
[
Har07
, Proposition 1.5]
and recalling that
S_{1}=S_{2}
for sheaves on curves, we note that a sheaf on
X
is
\omega
-reflexive if and only if it is

S_{1}
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}
is
\omega
-reflexive by
[
Har07
, Proposition 1.6]
and hence it is
S_{1}
. Then, by the universal property of
q
, there is a unique map
\psi:\mathcal{F}^{tf}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}
such that
\psi\circ q=\alpha(\mathcal{F})
. Note that

q^{\omega\omega}\circ\psi\circ q=q^{\omega\omega}\circ\alpha(\mathcal{F})=\alpha(\mathcal{F}^{tf})\circ q,

so by surjectivity of
q
we conclude that
q^{\omega\omega}\circ\psi=\alpha(\mathcal{F}^{tf})
.

We show moreover that
\psi
is an isomorphism. By construction
\mathcal{F}^{tf}
is pure, hence it is
\omega
-reflexive and
\alpha(\mathcal{F}^{tf})
is an isomorphism. Moreover,
q^{\omega\omega}
is surjective and generically an isomorphism since
q
is surjective and generically an isomorphism. Then, the kernel
K
of
q^{\omega\omega}
is a subsheaf of
\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}
which is generically zero. Since
\mathcal{F}^{\omega\omega}
is pure, we conclude that
K
is everywhere zero and then
q^{\omega\omega}
is an isomorphism. This shows that
\psi=(q^{\omega\omega})^{-1}\circ\alpha(\mathcal{F}^{tf})
is an isomorphism.
∎

In order to study the formula for
[\pi_{*}]
, we study a preliminary formula for
\pi_{*}
in the case of effective generalized divisors.

Lemma 2.3.9
.

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
be an effective generalized divisor with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Then, there is an injection:

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\hookrightarrow\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}

whose image in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is the
0
-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

Consider the short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D}\rightarrow 0.

Since
\pi
is finite and flat, the pushforward induces a a short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})\rightarrow 0.

In particular, the last exact sequence is a finite presentation of
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
whose middle term is locally free. Hence, by Definition
2.3.1
, the
0
-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
is equal to the image of the morphism:

\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\otimes\det\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\big)^{-1}\xrightarrow{\hskip 4.09723pt\det\varphi\otimes 1\hskip 4.09723pt}\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}.

Consider now the determinant map
\det\varphi
. Since
\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
is also a pure sheaf, applying the universal property of the torsion-free quotient together with Lemma
2.3.8
we obtain the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

The canonical map
\alpha
is surjective by Lemma
2.3.8
. Since
\mathcal{I}
is locally free of rank 1 at the generic points of
X
, both
\det\phi
and
\alpha
are generically isomorphisms; hence, the map
\beta
is generically an isomorphism. Since its domain
\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}
is pure, we conclude that its kernel is zero, hence
\beta
is injective. We have then factorized
\det\varphi
as the composition of a surjective map
\alpha
followed by an injective map
\beta
.

Tensoring by
\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}
, we obtain then the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\varphi\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\otimes 1}

The map
\alpha\otimes 1
is surjective, so the composition
\eta\circ(\beta\otimes 1)
is an injective map whose image in
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is equal to the image of the map
\eta\circ(\det\varphi\otimes 1)
. By the previous remark, such image coincides with the
0
-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{I})
, proving the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to give a sheaf-theoretic formula for
[\pi_{*}]
. Recall that, for any curve
X
, the set
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
can be seen as the set of isomorphism classes of generalized line bundles on on
X
.

Proposition 2.3.10
.

(Formula for the direct image of generalized line bundles)

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a generalized line bundle on
X
. Then,

[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L})\simeq\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

Proof.

By the surjectivity of
\operatorname{GDiv}(X)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{GPic}(X)
, we can pick a generalized divisor
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
with fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}
isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}
; then,

[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L})=[\pi_{*}]([D])=[\pi_{*}(D)]

by Definition
2.3.7
. By Lemma
2.2.3
, there are effective generalized divisors
E,F
on
X
such that
D=E-F
and
F
Cartier. Denote with
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
the ideal sheaf of
E
and with
\mathcal{J}
the ideal sheaf of
F
. Since
F
is Cartier, the condition
D=E-F
can be rewritten as
E=D+F
, or in terms of sheaves:

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J}.

Consider the direct images of
E
and
F
. By Lemma
2.3.9
, the ideal sheaf of
\pi_{*}(E)
is isomorphic to

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1},

while the ideal sheaf of
\pi_{*}(F)
is isomorphic to

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\simeq\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

By Definition
2.3.5
,
\pi_{*}(D)=\pi_{*}(E)-\pi_{*}(F)
. Then, the fractional ideal of
\pi_{*}(D)
is isomorphic to:

\displaystyle\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)^{-1}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}),

which in turn is isomorphic to:

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)^{-1}.

Then, we are left to prove that:

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)^{-1}\simeq\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1},

or, equivalently, that:

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right),

under the assumptions
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}
locally principal. Consider an open cover
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
Y
such that
\mathcal{J}
is trivial on each
U_{i}=\pi^{-1}(V_{i})
, i.e. there is an isomorphism
\lambda_{i}:\mathcal{J}_{|U_{i}}=(f_{i})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}

for each
i\in I
. On the intersections
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, the collection
\{\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}^{-1}\}
of automorphisms of
{\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{|U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
is a cochain of elements of
{\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}}_{|U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
that measures the obstruction for the
\lambda_{i}
’s to glue to a global isomorphism. We define now an isomorphism

\alpha:\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)

by glueing a collection of isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}
defined on each
V_{i}
. To do so, we define each
\alpha_{i}
as the following composition of arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\right)_{|V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}\big)\right)_{|V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}_{|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{I}_{|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigwedge^{n}(\pi_{*}\lambda_{i})\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes\left(\bigwedge^{n}(\pi_{*}\lambda_{i}^{-1})\right)^{\omega\omega}}

i.e.
\alpha_{i}:=\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\lambda_{i}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\left(\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}
. Since
\bigwedge^{n}\big(\pi_{*}\_\big)
and
(\_)^{\omega\omega}
are functorial, the obstruction
\alpha_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}^{-1}
is trivial on any
V_{i}\cap V_{j}
, whence the
\alpha_{i}
’s glue together to a global isomorphism
\alpha
.
∎

Corollary 2.3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a line bundle on
X
. Then,

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})=[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L}).

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.7
,

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

On the other side, by Proposition
2.3.10
,

[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L})\simeq\left(\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is locally free,
\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})
is a line bundle and in particular is a pure coherent sheaf. Then,
\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})\simeq\left(\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L})\right)^{\omega\omega}
, proving the thesis.
∎

Corollary 2.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a generalized line bundle on
X
. Suppose that
Y
is smooth. Then:

[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L})\simeq\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

Proof.

First note that, by Proposition
2.3.10
,

[\pi_{*}](\mathcal{L})\simeq\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\otimes\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}.

Second, observe that the pure sheaf
\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}
is locally free of rank
n
since
Y
is smooth (see
[
HL10
, Example 1.1.16]
), and
\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big)
is a line bundle. Then,

\left(\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big)\right)^{\omega\omega}\simeq\textstyle{\bigwedge^{n}}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}\big).

Combining these two facts, we have proved the thesis.
∎

2.3.3.
The inverse image

In this subsection, we define the inverse image for generalized divisors and generalized line bundles and we study the relation of the inverse image with the direct image. We start from the case of effective divisors.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.13
.

(Inverse image of an effective generalized divisor)
Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(Y)
be an effective generalized divisor with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. The
inverse image of
D
relative to
\pi
, denoted
\pi^{*}(D)
, is the effective generalized divisor with ideal sheaf
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Proof.

The inverse image ideal
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})
is an ideal sheaf of
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})
-modules and can be extended to
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules via the canonical map of sheaves of rings
\pi^{\sharp}:\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
. It is coherent since
\mathcal{I}
is coherent. If
\eta
is a generic point of
X
, then
\pi(\eta)
is a generic point of
Y
, hence

(\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\eta}=\mathcal{I}_{\pi(\eta)}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X,\eta}=\mathcal{O}_{X,\eta}

since
\mathcal{I}_{\pi(\eta)}=\mathcal{O}_{Y,\pi(\eta)}
.
∎

Remark 2.3.14
.

In the setting of Definition
2.3.13
, consider the short exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D}\rightarrow 0.

Since
\pi
is flat and surjective, the pullback functor
\_\otimes_{\pi^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is exact as well as the inverse image functor
\pi^{-1}
. Then, the previous exact sequence induces the following exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{D}\rightarrow 0.

Since
\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, the pullback sheaf
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{I})
has a canonical injection in
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, whose image is exactly the inverse image ideal
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}

Lemma 2.3.15
.

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(Y)
be a generalized effective divisor and
E\in\operatorname{CDiv}^{+}(Y)
be an effective Cartier divisor on
Y
. Then, the inverse image divisor
\pi^{*}(E)
is Cartier and
\pi^{*}(D+E)=\pi^{*}(D)+\pi^{*}(E)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the ideal sheaves of
D
and
E
respectively. The ideal sheaf
\mathcal{J}
is locally principal, hence its inverse image
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{J})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}
is again locally principal; then,
\pi^{*}(E)
is Cartier. The generalized divisor
D+E
is defined by the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J}
, whose inverse image is:

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{J})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}

\displaystyle=(\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}=

\displaystyle=(\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X})\cdot(\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{J})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X}),

which is the defining ideal of
\pi^{*}(D)+\pi^{*}(E)
.
∎

Now, we can extend the definition of inverse image to any generalized divisor and study its properties.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.16
.

(Inverse image of a generalized divisor)
Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(Y)
be any generalized divisor and let
D=E-F
with
E,F
effective generalized divisors and
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
. The
inverse image of
D
relative to
\pi
, denoted
\pi^{*}(D)
, is the generalized divisor
\pi^{*}(E)-\pi^{*}(F)
.

Proof.

To prove that it is well defined, let
D=D^{\prime}-E=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}-\widetilde{E}
, with
D^{\prime},\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
effective and
E,\widetilde{E}
effective Cartier.
Since
E,\widetilde{E}
are Cartier,
D^{\prime}+\widetilde{E}=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}+E
; then, by Lemma
2.3.15
we have:

\pi^{*}(D^{\prime})+\pi^{*}(\widetilde{E})=\pi^{*}(\widetilde{D}^{\prime})+\pi^{*}(E).

Again by Lemma
2.3.15

\pi^{*}(E)
and
\pi^{*}(\widetilde{E})
are Cartier, so they can be subtracted from each side in order to obtain:

\pi^{*}(D^{\prime})-\pi^{*}(E)=\pi^{*}(\widetilde{D}^{\prime})-\pi^{*}(\widetilde{E}),

so
\pi^{*}(D)
does not depend on the choice of
D^{\prime}
and
E
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.17
.

(Properties of inverse image)

(1)

Let
D\in\operatorname{CDiv}(Y)
be a Cartier divisor on
Y
. Then,
\pi^{*}(D)
is a Cartier divisor and
\pi^{*}(-D)=-\pi^{*}(D)
. Moreover,
\pi^{*}(D)
coincides with
\pi^{*}(D)
of Definition
2.1.3
.

(2)

Let
D,E\in\operatorname{GDiv}(Y)
be generalized divisors, such that
E
is Cartier. Then,
\pi^{*}(D+E)=\pi^{*}(D)+\pi^{*}(E)
.

(3)

Let
V\subset Y
be an open subset, and denote with
\pi_{U}
the restriction of
\pi
to
U=\pi^{-1}(V)\subset X
. Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(Y)
be a generalized divisor on
Y
. Then,

(\pi_{U})^{*}(D_{|V})=\pi^{*}(D)_{|U}

(4)

Let
D,D^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GDiv}(Y)
be generalized divisors such that
D\sim D^{\prime}
. Then,
\pi^{*}(D)\sim\pi^{*}(D^{\prime})
.

Proof.

To prove (1), consider
D=E-F
with
E,F
effective and
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
on
Y
. Since
D
is Cartier and
E=D+F
, then also
E
is Cartier. By Definition
2.3.16
,

\pi^{*}(D)=\pi^{*}(E)-\pi^{*}(F).

By Lemma
2.3.15
, it is a difference of Cartier divisors and hence it is Cartier. To compute
\pi(-D)
, note that since
F
is Cartier then
-D=F-E
, and it is a difference of effective Cartier divisors; then, apply Definition
2.3.16
to obtain

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(-D)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}(F)-\pi^{*}(E)=

\displaystyle=-\pi^{*}(D).

To compare
\pi^{*}(D)
with Definition
2.1.3
, let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
be the ideal sheaves of
E
and
F
and let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an open cover of
Y
such that
\mathcal{I}_{|V_{i}}and

\mathcal{J}_{|V_{i}}
are principal ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{Y|V_{i}}
-modules generated by regular sections
s_{i}
and
t_{i}
of
\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{O}_{Y})
, respectively on each
i\in I
. The fractional ideal of
D
is generated on each
V_{i}
by the meromorphic regular section
u_{i}=s_{i}/t_{i}
of
\Gamma(V_{i},\mathcal{K}_{Y})
. By Definition
2.3.13
, the ideal sheaves of
\pi^{*}(E)
and
\pi^{*}(F)
are generated on each
U_{i}=\pi^{-1}(V_{i})
by
\pi_{U_{i}}^{\sharp}(s_{i})
and
\pi_{U_{i}}^{\sharp}(t_{i})
respectively. Then, by Definition
2.3.16
, the fractional ideal of
\pi^{*}(D)
is generated on each
U_{i}
by the meromorphic regular section
\pi_{U_{i}}^{\sharp}(s_{i})/\pi_{U_{i}}^{\sharp}(t_{i})
of
\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{K}_{X})
. These are exactly the local generators for
\pi^{*}(D)
as defined in Definition
2.1.3
.

To prove (2), consider
D=D_{1}-D_{2}
and
E=E_{1}-E_{2}
, with
D_{1},D_{2},E_{1},E_{2}
effective and
D_{2},E_{2}
Cartier. Note that
D+E=(D_{1}+E_{1})-(D_{2}+E_{2})
, and it is a difference of effective divisors with
D_{2}+E_{2}
Cartier. Then, applying Definition
2.3.16
and Lemma
2.3.15
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(D+E)

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}((D_{1}+E_{1})-(D_{2}+E_{2}))=

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}(D_{1}+E_{1})-\pi^{*}(D_{2}+E_{2})=

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}(D_{1})+\pi^{*}(E_{1})-\pi^{*}(D_{2})-\pi^{*}(E_{2})=

\displaystyle=(\pi^{*}(D_{1})-\pi^{*}(D_{2}))-(\pi^{*}(E_{1})-\pi^{*}(E_{2}))=

\displaystyle=\pi^{*}(D)+\pi^{*}(E).

To prove (3), if
D
is effective, the result follows the fact that the inverse image functor
\pi^{-1}
commutes with restrictions. Then, observe that the operations of product and inverse of fractional ideals also commute with restrictions.

To prove (4), let
f\in\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{K}_{Y})
be a global section that generates a principal divisor
E=(f)\in\operatorname{Prin}(Y)
such that
D=D^{\prime}+E
. The inverse image
\pi^{*}(E)
is the principal divisor
(\pi^{\sharp}(f))\in\operatorname{Prin}(X)
. Then, by Lemma
2.3.15
,
\pi^{*}(D)=\pi^{*}(D^{\prime})+(\pi^{\sharp}(f))
; so
\pi^{*}(D)
and
\pi^{*}(D^{\prime})
are linearly equivalent.
∎

We are now ready to define the inverse image for generalized line bundles.

Definition/Lemma 2.3.18
.

(Inverse image for generalized line bundles)
The inverse image for generalized divisors induce a inverse image map between the sets of generalized line bundles, defined as:

\displaystyle[\pi^{*}]:\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{GPic}(Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{GPic}(X)
