\displaystyle[D]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\pi^{*}(D)].

Proof.

Recall that for any curve
X
, the set
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
can be seen equivalently as the set of generalized line bundles or as the set of generalized divisors modulo linear equivalence. Here,
[\pi^{*}]
is defined in terms of generalized divisors modulo linear equivalence. By Proposition
2.3.17
, the direct images of linearly equivalent divisors are linearly equivalent, hence
[\pi^{*}]
is well defined.
∎

Remark 2.3.19
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a generalized line bundle on
Y
and
D
is a generalized divisor with fractional ideal
\mathcal{I}
isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}
, then the inverse image divisor
\pi^{*}(D)
has fractional ideal isomorphic to the pullback sheaf
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{I})
by Remark
2.3.14
. Since
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{I})
is isomorphic to
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{L})
as abstract
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, we conclude that the inverse image
[\pi^{*}](\mathcal{L})
of the generalized line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is equal to the pullback sheaf
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{L})
.

We prove now a result on the composition of the direct image with the inverse image of generalized divisors.

Proposition 2.3.20
.

(Composition of the direct image with the inverse image)

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(Y)
be a generalized divisor on
Y
. Then,

\pi_{*}(\pi^{*}(D))=n\cdot D.

Proof.

Since both of the terms are linear with respect to the sum of Cartier divisor, we can suppose that
D
is effective with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. First note that, from the exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{D}\rightarrow 0

together with Remark
2.3.14
, we obtain
\mathcal{O}_{X}/(\pi^{-1}\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I}=\pi^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}/\mathcal{I})
. To prove the thesis, we show that the equality

\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\big(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})\big)=\mathcal{I}^{n}

holds locally around any point
y\in Y
. Let
V\subseteq Y
be an open neighborhood of
y
such that
(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})_{|V}\simeq(\mathcal{O}_{Y|V})^{\oplus n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{|V}
is generated by sections
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}
of
\Gamma(V,\mathcal{I})
. Then, consider the following presentation:

\mathcal{O}_{Y|V}^{\oplus r}\xrightarrow{(\cdot s_{1},\dots,\cdot s_{r})}\mathcal{O}_{Y|V}\rightarrow(\mathcal{O}_{Y}/\mathcal{I})_{|V}\rightarrow 0.

Pulling back with
\pi^{*}
, we obtain the following exact sequence on
U=\pi^{-1}(V)
:

\mathcal{O}_{X|U}^{\oplus r}\xrightarrow{(\cdot s_{1},\dots,\cdot s_{r})}\mathcal{O}_{X|U}\rightarrow(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})_{|U}\rightarrow 0.

In order to compute
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\big(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})\big)_{|V}
, we consider then the pushforward sequence:

\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\oplus r}\big)_{|V}\xrightarrow{(\cdot s_{1},\dots,\cdot s_{r})}\big(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\big)_{|V}\rightarrow\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})_{|V}\rightarrow 0.

Since
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})_{|V}\simeq\big(\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{\oplus n}\big)_{|V}
, the map on the left is represented by the following
n\times nr
matrix with entries in
\Gamma(V,\mathcal{O}_{Y})
:

M=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\begin{matrix}s_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&s_{1}\end{matrix}&\dots&\begin{matrix}s_{n}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&s_{n}\end{matrix}\end{array}\right].

Now,
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\big(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})\big)_{|V}
is generated by the
n\times n
minors of
M
, i.e. all the possible products of
n
generators of
\mathcal{I}
on
V
, with ripetitions. This shows that
\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}\big(\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\pi^{*}\mathcal{I})\big)_{|V}=\mathcal{I}_{|V}^{n}
.
∎

2.3.4.
Relation with the degree

In this subsection we show that
\pi_{*}
preserves the degree of divisors under the condition that
Y
is smooth over
k
. In general, however, the direct image of a generalized divisor
D
may not have the same degree of
D
, as the following example shows. Since we are dealing with degrees, we
assume that
X
and
Y
are projective curves over a base field

k
.

Example 2.3.21
.

Fix
k
an algebraically closed field. Let
A=k[x,y]/(y^{2}-x^{4})
be the affine coordinate ring of a curve
X=\operatorname{Spec}A
with a tacnode at the point
P
corresponding to the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}=(x,y)
.

The involution
\sigma
on
A
defined by
x\mapsto-x,y\mapsto y
induces a involution
\sigma_{X}
on the curve
X
. The geometric quotient
Y=X/\sigma_{X}
is an affine curve with coordinate ring equal to the ring of invariants
A^{\sigma}=k[x^{2},y]/(y^{2}-x^{4})
, that is isomorphic to
B=k[s,t]/(t^{2}-s^{2})
putting
s\mapsto x^{2}
and
t\mapsto y
. The quotient curve
Y
has a simple node at the point
Q
corresponding to the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{q}=(s,t)
.

The inclusion map
A^{\sigma}\subset A
gives to
A
the structure of free
B
-module, with basis
\{1,x\}
; so, the corresponding morphism of curve
\pi:X\rightarrow Y=X/\sigma_{X}
is a finite, locally free map of degree
2
sending
P
to
Q
.

Let
D
be the generalized divisor on
X
defined by the ideal
I=(x^{2},y)\subset A
; note that
D
is supported only on the tacnode
P
. Since we want to compare
\deg(D)=\deg_{P}(D)
with
\deg(\pi_{*}(D))=\deg_{Q}(\pi_{*}(D))
, we can restrict to work locally around
P
and
Q
. Let
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the local ring of
Y
at
Q
. The induced map
B_{\mathfrak{q}}\rightarrow A_{\mathfrak{p}}
makes
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
a free
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module of rank 2.

Let
E=A_{\mathfrak{p}}/I_{\mathfrak{p}}\simeq k[x]/(x^{2})
be the local ring of
D
at the
P
. We have:

\deg_{P}(D)=\ell(E)=2.

Observe that
E
has the following free presentation as
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module:

A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\oplus 2}\xrightarrow{(\cdot x^{2},\cdot y)}A_{\mathfrak{p}}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0.

Since
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a free
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module of rank 2,
E
has also a presentation as
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module:

B_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\oplus 4}\xrightarrow{\varphi}B_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\oplus 2}\rightarrow E\rightarrow 0

where

\varphi=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}s&0&t&0\\
0&s&0&t\end{array}\right].

Then, the 0-th Fitting ideal of
E
as
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module is
F_{0}(E)=(s^{2},t^{2},st)\subset B_{\mathfrak{q}}
. We have:

\deg_{Q}(\pi_{*}(D))=\ell(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/F_{0}(E))=3.

Note that there are divisors of degree 2 on
X
, supported at
P
, whose direct image has degree
2
on
Y
. For example, take
D^{\prime}=(x)
.

Remark 2.3.22
.

The previous example shows, in particular, that Proposition 2.33 in
[
Vas04
]
is false.

We now prove that the degree is preserved if the direct image is Cartier. We show first a proposition that computes the degree at any point where the direct image is locally principal.

Proposition 2.3.23
.

(Degree of the direct image of a generalized divisor)

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be a generalized divisor on
X
and let
y\in Y
be a point in codimension 1 of the support of
\pi_{*}(D)
. Suppose that
\pi_{*}(D)
is locally principal at
y
. Then,

\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D).

Proof.

First, suppose that
D
is effective. Let
V=\operatorname{Spec}(B)
be an affine open neighborhood of
y
with affine pre-image
U=\pi^{-1}(V)=\operatorname{Spec}(A)
, and let
I\subset A
denote the ideal of
D
restricted to
U
. The coordinate ring of
D
on
U
is the Artin ring
A/I
whose spectrum is equal to
\operatorname{Spec}(A)\cap\operatorname{Supp}(D)=\{\mathfrak{p}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{p}_{s}\}
; hence we have:

A/I=\prod_{i=1}^{s}(A/I)_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}.

Let
\mathfrak{q}\subset B
denote the maximal ideal corresponding to
y
in
\operatorname{Spec}(B)
and let
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the associated local ring of dimension 1. Then, the localization of
A/I
at
\mathfrak{q}
, denoted
E
, is the coordinate ring of
D
restricted to the fiber of
y
:

E=(A/I)_{\mathfrak{q}}=\prod_{\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{i})=\mathfrak{q}}(A/I)_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}.

Since
\pi_{*}(D)
is effective, the degree of
\pi_{*}(D)
at
y
is:

\displaystyle\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\ell\big(\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}/F_{0}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D})_{y}\big)[\kappa(y):k]=

\displaystyle=\ell\big(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/F_{0}(E)\big)[\kappa(y):k].

By hypothesis
F_{0}(E)
is an invertible module, so by
[
Vas04
, Proposition 2.32]
, we have
\ell\big(B_{\mathfrak{q}}/F_{0}(E)\big)=\ell(E)
.
On the other hand, thanks to
[
Sta19
,
\href
https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/02M0Tag 02M0]
,
note that:

\displaystyle\ell(E)

\displaystyle=\ell\left(\prod_{\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{i})=\mathfrak{q}}(A/I)_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}_{i})=\mathfrak{q}}\ell(A_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}})][k(\mathfrak{p}_{i}):k(\mathfrak{q})]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\ell\big(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathcal{I}_{x}\big)[\kappa(x):\kappa(y)]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)[\kappa(x):\kappa(y)].

Putting everything together we have:

\displaystyle\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\ell(E)[\kappa(y):k]=

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)[\kappa(x):\kappa(y)]\right)[\kappa(y):k]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)[\kappa(x):k]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)

for
D
effective generalized divisor on
X
.

Let now
D=E-F
be a generalized divisor, written as a difference of effective generalized divisors with
F
Cartier by Lemma
2.2.3
. Since
\pi_{*}(F)
is Cartier, using the result for effective divisors estabilished before, we have:

\displaystyle\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(E)-\pi_{*}(F))=

\displaystyle=\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(E))-\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(F))=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\left[\deg_{x}(E)-\deg_{x}(F)\right]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D).

∎

Corollary 2.3.24
.

Let
D\in\operatorname{GDiv}(X)
be a generalized divisor on
X
such that
\pi_{*}(D)
is Cartier. Then,

\deg_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))=\deg_{X}(D).

Proof.

Applying Definition
2.2.4
and Proposition
2.3.23
, we get:

\displaystyle\deg_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))=

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D).

By the properties of the Fitting image,
\pi_{*}(D)
is supported on the set-theoretic image of the support of
D
; hence, the
x
appearing in the last sum are all the
x
for which
\deg_{x}(D)
is not zero. Then, the previous sum gives:

\displaystyle\deg_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{x}(D)=\deg_{X}(D).

∎

Corollary 2.3.25
.

Suppose that
Y
is smooth. Then, for any generalized divisor
D
on
X
,

\deg_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))=\deg_{X}(D).

Proof.

Let
D
be a generalized divisor on
X
. The direct image
\pi_{*}(D)
is a generalized divisor on
Y
smooth, hence Cartier. Then, apply Corollary
2.3.24
.
∎

Proposition
2.3.23
yields another useful corollary about the surjectivity of the direct image morphism.

Corollary 2.3.26
.

(Surjectivity of the direct image for effective divisors)

Suppose that
Y
is smooth and
k
is algebraically closed. Then, the direct image for effective divisors:

\pi_{*}^{+}:\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{CDiv}^{+}(Y)

is surjective.

Proof.

Let
E\in\operatorname{CDiv}^{+}(Y)
be an effective Cartier divisor on
Y
and let
V=\operatorname{Spec}(R)\subseteq Y
be an affine open subset of
Y
such that
E
is supported on
V
. Let
\operatorname{Supp}(E)=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\}
be the support of
E
and for each
i=1,\dots,r
let

d_{i}=\deg_{y_{i}}(E)/[\kappa(y_{i}):k]=\ell_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y_{i}}}(\mathcal{O}_{E,y_{i}}).

Let
U=\operatorname{Spec}(S)=\pi^{-1}(V)
be the preimage of
V
. For each
i
, pick one element
x_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(y_{i})
in the finite fiber of
y_{i}
and let
\mathfrak{M}_{i}
be the maximal ideal of
S
corresponding to the point
x_{i}
. Then, the ideal
I=\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdot\dots\cdot\mathfrak{M}_{r}^{d_{r}}
in
S
defines a divisor
D
on
U
(and hence of
X
) such that
\deg_{y_{i}}(E)=\deg_{x_{i}}(D)
. Looking at its direct image
\pi_{*}(D)
, it is an effective divisor on
Y
with the same support of
E
and the same degree at any point by Propostion
2.3.23
. Since
Y
is smooth, we conclude that
\pi_{*}(D)=E
.
∎

2.4.
The direct and inverse image

for families of generalized divisors

Let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a
finite, flat map of degree
n
between projective curves over a field
k
. In the present section, we discuss the definition of direct and inverse image for families of effective generalized divisors. Under suitable conditions, recalling Definition
2.2.7
, we aim to define a pair of geometric morphisms:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}

\displaystyle:\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hilb}_{Y}

\displaystyle\pi^{*}

\displaystyle:\operatorname{Hilb}_{Y}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}

that, on
k
-valued points, coincide with the direct and inverse image between
\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(X)
and
\operatorname{GDiv}^{+}(Y)
.

Remark 2.4.1
.

Giving a definition of the direct image for families of effective generalized divisors is not possible in general when the curve
Y
is not smooth over
k
. Consider, for example, the setting of Example
2.3.21
; since
X
and
Y
are reduced curves with planar singularities, their Hilbert schemes of generalized divisors of given degree are connected (see
[
AIK77
]
,
[
BGS81
]
). The effective divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
on
X
defined by
(x^{2},y)
and
(x)
on
X
have both degree
2
, but their direct images on the quotient node
Y
have degree respectively equal to
3
and
2
. Then, their
k
-points
D_{1},D_{2}
in the connected component
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{2}
of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
are sent to different connected components of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{Y}
. This shows that
the direct image of divisors in general is not defined as a geometric map
.

In the rest of the section, consider
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
a finite, flat map of degree
n
between projective curves over
k
, and
suppose that
Y
is smooth over
k
. Recall that, in such case,
\operatorname{Hilb}_{Y}=\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}
.

Definition/Lemma 2.4.2
.

(Direct image for families of effective generalized divisors)
Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. The
direct image map for the Hilbert scheme of effective generalized divisors
is defined on the
T
-valued points as:

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(T):\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}(T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}(T)

\displaystyle D\subseteq X\times_{k}T

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}\left(\operatorname{Fitt}_{0}(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{O}_{D}))\right)

where
\pi_{T}:X\times_{k}T\rightarrow Y\times_{k}T
is the morphism induced by base change of
\pi
.

Proof.

Let
D\subseteq X\times_{k}T
be a
T
-flat family of effective divisors of
X
, defined by an ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{D}=\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}/\mathcal{I}
is flat over
S
. From the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D}\rightarrow 0

we deduce that also
\mathcal{I}
is flat over
T
. Since
\pi
is finite and flat,
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I})
is also flat over
T
, fiberwise locally free since
Y
is smooth and hence locally free on
Y\times_{k}T
by
[
HL10
, Lemma 2.1.7]
. Moreover, it fits the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I})\xrightarrow{\varphi}\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T})\rightarrow\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{O}_{D})\rightarrow 0.

Then, by Definition
2.3.1
, the
0
-th Fitting ideal of
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{O}_{D})
is the image of the canonical injection

\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}\xhookrightarrow{\det(\varphi)}\mathcal{O}_{Y\times_{k}T}

and this is locally free over
Y\times_{k}T
, hence flat over
T
. Then, it defines a
T
-flat family of effective divisors of
Y
.
∎

Remark 2.4.3
.

For any
T
-family of effective generalized divisors
D\subseteq X\times_{k}T
and for any point
t\in T
, the fiber
\pi_{*}(T)(D)_{t}
is equal to the direct image
\pi_{*}(D_{t})
defined for the effective divisor
D_{t}
on
X
. Moreover, since
Y
is smooth, by Corollary
2.3.25
we have:

\deg_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D_{t}))=\deg_{X}(D_{t}).

Then, for any
d\geq 0
,
\pi_{*}
restricts to a map:

\pi_{*}^{d}:\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}\longrightarrow\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}^{d}.

Definition/Lemma 2.4.4
.

(Inverse image for families of effective generalized divisors)
Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. The
inverse image map for the Hilbert scheme of effective Cartier divisors
is defined on the
T
-valued points as:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(T):\hskip 20.00003pt\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}(T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}(T)

\displaystyle D\subseteq Y\times_{k}T

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}\left(\pi_{T}^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{D})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)

where
\pi_{T}:X\times_{k}T\rightarrow Y\times_{k}T
is the morphism induced by base change of
\pi
and
\mathcal{I}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{Y\times_{k}T}
is the ideal sheaf of
D
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{I}
is locally principal,
\pi_{T}^{-1}(\mathcal{I})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}
is locally principal. The restriction of
\pi^{*}(T)(D)
to the fiber over any
t\in T
has ideal sheaf
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{t})\cdot\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}t}
, which is equal to the defining ideal of
\pi^{*}(D_{t})
by Definition
2.3.16
. Hence,
\pi^{*}(T)(D)
is a locally principal subscheme of
X\times_{k}T
, such that all fibers over
T
are effective Cartier divisors by Lemma
2.3.15
. Then,
\pi^{*}(T)(D)
is
T
-flat by
[
Sta19
,
\href
https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/062YTag 062Y]
, hence it is a
T
-family of Cartier divisors.
∎

Remark 2.4.5
.

For any integer
d\geq 0
,
\pi^{*}
restricts to a map:

\pi^{*}_{d}:\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}^{d}\longrightarrow\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{nd}.

We study now some properties of the direct and inverse image for families of effective generalized divisors. With a slight abuse of notation, we will write
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
instead of
\pi_{*}(T)
and
\pi^{*}(T)
, when it is clear that we are working on
T
-points.

Proposition 2.4.6
.

(Properties of the direct and inverse image for families of effective generalized divisors)

Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. Then, the following fact holds.

(1)

Let
D,E
be
T
-families of effective divisors over
X
such that
E
is a family of Cartier divisors. Then,
\pi_{*}(D+E)=\pi_{*}(D)+\pi_{*}(E)
.

(2)

Let
F,G
be
T
-families of effective divisors over
Y
. Then,
\pi^{*}(F+G)=\pi^{*}(F)+\pi^{*}(G)
.

(3)

If
F
is a
T
-family of effective divisors over
Y
, then
\pi_{*}(\pi^{*}(F))=nF
.

Proof.

The proof of parts (1) and (2) follows the proofs of the second part of Lemma
2.3.4
and
2.3.15
respectively. The proof of part (3) follows the proof of Proposition
2.3.20
.
∎

2.5.
The Norm and the inverse image

for families of torsion-free rank-1 sheaves

In the present section, we provide the definition of the Norm map for families torsion-free sheaves of rank 1 and the related inverse image map. Since we are dealing with families of sheaves, we
assume that
X
and
Y
are projective curves over a field
k
.

In order to be compatible with the direct image for generalized line bundles, our definition of the Norm map on
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
will be inspired by the sheaf-theoretic formula of Proposition
2.1.7
. By Proposition
2.3.10
and its corollaries, the generalization of such formula to generalized divisors and torsion-free sheaves involves taking the double
\omega
-dual of the exterior power of the pushforward of generalized line bundles. In general, this operation does not behave well in families if
Y
is not smooth.

Then, in accordance with the previous section, we
suppose that
Y
is smooth over
k
; in such case, the moduli space
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(Y)
is actually equal to the Jacobian
J(Y)
. Then, the aim of this chapter is to provide a pair of geometric morphism:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}:\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)

\displaystyle\rightarrow J(Y)

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}:J(Y)

\displaystyle\rightarrow J(X)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)

that, on
k
-valued points, coincide with the direct and inverse image maps between
\operatorname{GPic}(X)
and
\operatorname{GPic}(Y)=\operatorname{Pic}(Y)
.

Recall also (Definition
2.2.8
) that, for any line bundle
M
on
X
, the compactified Jacobian of
X
is related to the Hilbert scheme of effective generalized divisors via the twisted Abel map
\mathcal{A}_{M}
. We will show that the direct image map and the Norm map are compatible as well as the inverse image maps, meaning that for any
M\in J(X)
and
N\in J(Y)
there are commutative diagrams of
k
-schemes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(X).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\pi^{*}(N)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}

We give first the definition for the Norm map on
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
.

Definition/Lemma 2.5.1
.

(Norm map for torsion-free rank-1 sheaves)
Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. The
Norm map between compactified Jacobians
associated to
\pi
is defined on the
T
-valued points as:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(T):\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)(T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J(Y)(T)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a
T
-family of torsion-free sheaves of rank 1 on
X
, i.e. a
T
-flat coherent sheaf on
X\times_{k}T
, whose fibers over
T
are torsion-free sheaves of rank 1. The push-forward
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})
is a
T
-flat coherent sheaf on
Y\times_{k}T
such that, for any
t\in T
, the fiber
(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{L})_{t}
equals
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{t})
on
Y\simeq Y\times_{k}{t}
, . Since
Y
is smooth,
\pi_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{t})
is a locally free sheaf of rank
n
for any
t\in T
. Then, by
[
HL10
, Lemma 2.1.7]
,
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})
is a locally sheaf of rank
n
on
Y\times_{k}T
. Its determinant bundle is a line bundle on
Y\times_{k}T
, hence flat over
T
.
∎

The definition of the inverse image is standard but we give it for the sake of completeness.

Definition/Lemma 2.5.2
.

(Inverse image map for line bundles)
Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. The
inverse image map between Jacobians
associated to
\pi
is defined on the
T
-valued points as:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(T):\hskip 20.00003ptJ(Y)(T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J(X)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)(T)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\pi_{T}^{*}(\mathcal{N}).

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\mathcal{N}
is a
T
-flat coherent sheaf on
Y\times_{k}T
that is a line bundle on any fiber over
T
; then, by
[
HL10
, Lemma 2.1.7]
, it is a line bundle on
Y\times_{k}T
. We conclude that
\pi^{*}\mathcal{N}
is a line bundle on
X\times_{k}T
and hence a
T
-flat family of line bundles over
T
.
∎

Remark 2.5.3
.

When there is no ambiguity, we will write
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}
and
\pi^{*}
instead of
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(T)
and
\pi^{*}(T)
. The Norm and the inverse image for generalized line bundles define morphisms of algebraic stacks

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}:\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J(Y)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}:\hskip 20.00003ptJ(Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J(X).

For any integer
d
, they restricts to:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{d}:\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J^{d}(Y)

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{d}:\hskip 20.00003ptJ^{d}(Y)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J^{nd}(X).

Moreover, the Norm for torsion-free rank-1 sheaves, restricted to the locus of line bundles
J(X)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
, coincides with the classical Norm map from
J(X)
to
J(Y)
of Definition
2.1.9
.

We study now some properties of the Norm map on
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
and the inverse image map. First, we need a technical lemma.

Lemma 2.5.4
.

Let
T
be a fixed
k
-scheme. For any
T
-flat family
\mathcal{L}
of torsion-free rank-1 sheaves on
X\times_{k}T
and any line bundle
\mathcal{M}
on
X\times_{k}T
, there is an isomorphism:

\displaystyle\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{M})\right).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the second part of the proof of Proposition
2.3.10
.

Since
\pi_{T}:X\times_{k}T\rightarrow Y\times_{k}T
is finite and flat,
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})
is a
T
-flat coherent sheaf on
Y\times_{k}T
, that is locally free on any fiber over
T
since
Y
is smooth; then,
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})
is locally free of rank
n
by
[
HL10
, Lemma 2.1.7]
. The same holds for
\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})
. On the other hand, by
[
Gro61
, Proposition 6.1.12]
,
\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{M}
is a locally free
\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of rank
1
. In particular, there is open cover
\{V_{i}\}_{I}
of
Y
such that
\mathcal{M}
is a trivial
\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}
-module on each
U_{i}=\pi_{T}^{-1}(V_{i})
, i.e. there are isomorphisms
\lambda_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{|U_{i}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}
for each
i\in I
. On the intersections
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, the collection
\{\lambda_{i}\circ\lambda_{j}^{-1}\}
of automorphisms of
{\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}}_{|U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
is a cochain that measures the obstruction for the
\lambda_{i}
’s to glue to a global isomorphism. We define now an isomorphism

\alpha:\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)\longrightarrow\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{M})\right)

by glueing a collection of isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}
defined on each
V_{i}
. To do so, we define each
\alpha_{i}
as the following composition of arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\det\big(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\right)_{|V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{L}\big)\otimes\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{M}\big)\right)_{|V_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\big(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})_{|U_{i}}\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{L}_{|U_{i}}\big)\otimes\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{M}_{|U_{i}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{L}_{|U_{i}}\big)\otimes\det(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{L}_{|U_{i}}\big)\otimes\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X|U_{i}}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\lambda_{i}\big)\otimes\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\otimes\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\big)}

i.e.
\alpha_{i}:=\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\lambda_{i}\big)\otimes\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\lambda_{i}^{-1}\big)
. Since
\det\big(\pi_{T,*}\_\big)
is functorial, the obstruction
\alpha_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}^{-1}
is trivial on any
V_{i}\cap V_{j}
, whence the
\alpha_{i}
’s glue together to a global isomorphism
\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 2.5.5
.

(Properties of the Norm and the inverse image map)

Let
T
be any
k
-scheme. Then, the following facts hold.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)(T)
such that
\mathcal{M}
is a
T
-flat family of line bundles. Then,
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})\simeq\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\otimes\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M})
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\in J(Y)(T)
. Then,
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{\prime})\simeq\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N})\otimes\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime})
.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{N}\in J(Y)(T)
. Then,
\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N})
is a
T
-flat family of line bundles over
X
and
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N}))\simeq\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

To prove (1), note that
\mathcal{M}
is a
T
-flat coherent sheaf on
X\times_{k}T
that is a line bundle on any fiber over
T
; hence it is a line bundle on
X\times_{k}T
by
[
HL10
, Lemma 2.1.7]
. Then, applying Definition
2.5.1
and Lemma
2.5.4
, we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{L})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-2}\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\otimes\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M}).

Part (2) follows from the associative properties of the tensor product.

To prove (3), compute by Definitions
2.5.1
and
2.5.2
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N}))=\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\pi_{T}^{*}(\mathcal{N}))\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}.

By the projection formula
[
Sta19
,
\href
https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/01E8Tag 01E8]
and the standard properties of determinants, we have:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\pi^{*}(\mathcal{N}))

\displaystyle=\det\left(\mathcal{N}\otimes\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}^{n}\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}^{n}.

∎

We compare now the Norm and the inverse image maps between the compactified Jacobians with the direct and inverse image maps between the Hilbert schemes of divisors, respectively.

Proposition 2.5.6
.

(Comparison of the direct image and the Norm map via the Abel map)

For any line bundle
M
of degree
e
on
X
and for any
d\geq 0
, there is a commutative diagram of algebraic stacks over
k
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X,-d+e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,-d+e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{-d+e}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{M}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-d+e}}

Proof.

Let
T
be any
k
-scheme, let
D
be a
T
-flat family of effective divisors of degree
d
on
X
with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
, and denote with
\mathcal{M}
the pullback of
M
to
X\times_{k}T
. Following the bottom-left side of the square, combining Definitions
2.2.8
and
2.5.1
we get:

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-d+e}(\mathcal{A}_{M}^{d}(D))=\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-1}.

Following the top-right side of the square, combining definitions
2.4.2
,
2.5.1
and
2.2.8
we get:

\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}^{d}(\pi_{*}^{d}(D))=\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)^{-2}.

We are left to prove that:

\displaystyle\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{M})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}\mathcal{O}_{X\times_{k}T}\right)\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{I})\right)\otimes\det\left(\pi_{T,*}(\mathcal{M})\right).

Now,
\mathcal{I}
is a
T
-flat family of generalized line bundles by hypothesis and
\mathcal{M}
is a line bundle on
X\times_{k}T
since it is the pull-back of a line bundle on
X
. Then, the assertion is true by Lemma
2.5.4
.
∎

Proposition 2.5.7
.

(Comparison of the inverse image maps via the Abel map)

For any line bundle
N
of degree
e
on
Y
and any
d\geq 0
, there is a commutative diagram of algebraic stacks over
k
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(Y,-d+e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(X,-d+e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{N}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\pi^{*}(N)}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}_{-d+e}}

Proof.

Let
T
be any
k
-scheme, let
D
be a
T
-flat family of divisors of degree
d
on
Y
with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}\subseteq Y\times_{k}T
, and denote with
\mathcal{N}
the pullback of
N
to
Y\times_{k}T
. By Definition
2.2.8
, Proposition
2.5.5
(2) and Remark
2.3.19
, we have:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N}^{d}(D))

\displaystyle=\pi_{T}^{*}(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{N})\simeq\pi_{T}^{*}(\mathcal{I})\otimes\pi_{T}^{*}(\mathcal{N})\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\pi^{*}(N)}^{d}(\pi^{*}_{-d+e}(\mathcal{N})).

∎

Chapter 3
The fibers of the Norm map and the Prym stack

In the present chapter we study the fibers of the Norm map defined in Chapter
2
. The purpose is to generalize the Prym scheme associated to a finite, flat morphism between projective curves to the context of torsion-free sheaves of rank 1. Fix an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic 0. Let
X,Y
be projective curves over
k
, such that
Y
is smooth, and let
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite, flat morphism of degree
n
. We start by recalling the following definition.

Definition 3.0.1
.

The
Prym scheme
of
X
associated to
\pi
is the locus
\Pr(X,Y)\subseteq J(X)
of line bundles whose Norm with respect to
\pi
is trivial. In other words:

\Pr(X,Y)=\{\mathcal{L}\in J(X):\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Y}\}.

We can now extend the definition of the Prym stack to the context of torsion-free rank 1 sheaves using the Norm map defined in the previous chapter.

Definition 3.0.2
.

The
Prym stack
of
X
associated to
\pi
is the locus
\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
of torsion-free rank-1 sheaves whose Norm with respect to
\pi
is trivial. In other words:

\overline{\Pr}(X,Y):=\{(\mathcal{L},\lambda):\mathcal{L}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)\ \mathrm{and}\ \lambda:\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\}.

Remark 3.0.3
.

It follows from the definition that:

\Pr(X,Y)=\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)\cap J(X).

Since being locally-free is an open condition,
\Pr(X,Y)
is open in
\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)
.

The Prym stack is a fiber of the Norm map associated to
\pi
. In the present chapter, we study the fibers of the Norm map assuming that the curve
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities. The study of such fibers involves the study of the fibers of the direct image map for effective divisors defined in Chapter
2
, and the study of the fibers of the Hilbert-Chow morphism. We start from the last.

3.1.
The fibers of the Hilbert-Chow morphism

In this section, we refer to
[
Ber12
]
for notations and known results.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
X
be a projective curve over
k
and let
d
be a positive integer. The
d
-
symmetric power

X^{(d)}
of
X
is the quotient
X^{d}/\Sigma_{d}
of the Cartesian product
X^{n}
by the action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{d}
in
d
-letters permuting the factors. Note that, for
d=1
,
X^{(1)}=X
. For
d=0
, we put
X^{(0)}=\{0\}
.
The
scheme of effective
0
-cycles
(or of
effective Weil divisors
) of
X
is the algebraic scheme:

\operatorname{WDiv}^{+}(X)=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}X^{(d)}=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}\operatorname{WDiv}^{d}(X).

The
Hilbert-Chow morphism
of degree
d
associated to
X
is the map of schemes

\displaystyle\rho_{X}^{d}:\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}

\displaystyle\rightarrow X^{(d)}

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{x\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{x}(D)\cdot[x]

Denote with
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{d}
the restriction of
\rho_{X}^{d}
to the subscheme
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}^{d}\subseteq\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}^{d}
parametrizing Cartier divisors. The collection of Hilbert-Chow morphisms in non-negative degrees gives rise to the Hilbert-Chow morphism in any degree:

\rho_{X}:\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{WDiv}^{+}(X).

Finally, denote with
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}
the restriction of
\rho_{X}
to
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}\subseteq\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

If
X
is a smooth projective curve, then
\rho_{X}
is an isomorphism and coincides with
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}
.

We study now the fibers of the Hilbert-Chow morphism in the case when
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities.

Proposition 3.1.3
.

Suppose that
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities and that
Y
is smooth. Let
W\in\operatorname{WDiv}^{d}(X)
be an effective Weil divisor of degree
d
on
X
and let
\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)
be the corresponding fiber in
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
. The locus
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{-1}(W)
of Cartier divisors in
\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)
is an open and dense subset of
\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)
.

Proof.

First, note that
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{-1}(W)=\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
. Since
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}\subseteq\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
is open (see for example
[
MRV17a
, Fact 2.4]
),
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{-1}(W)
is open in
\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)
.

To prove that it is dense, let
W=\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}\cdot[x_{i}]
with the
x_{i}
’s are
s
distinct points. For each
i
, the fiber of
\rho_{X}
over the cycle
n_{i}\cdot[x_{i}]
is equal by definition to the punctual Hilbert scheme
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}
parametrizing
0
-dimensional subschemes of
X
supported at
x_{i}
having length
n_{i}
over
k
. Since the
x_{i}
are distinct, we have:

\displaystyle\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)

\displaystyle=\rho_{X}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}n_{i}\cdot[x_{i}]\right)=

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\rho_{X}^{-1}(n_{i}\cdot[x_{i}])=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\operatorname{Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}.

For each
i
, let
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}=\operatorname{Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
. An effective divisor is Cartier if and only if it is Cartier at each point of its support, hence:

\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{-1}(W)=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\left(\operatorname{Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{s}\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}.

For each
i
,
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}
is the smooth locus of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X,x_{i}}^{n_{i}}
by
[
BGS81
, Proposition 2.3]
, so it is dense; hence
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}\rho}_{X}^{-1}(W)
is dense in
\rho_{X}^{-1}(W)
.
∎

3.2.
The fibers of the direct image between Hilbert schemes

In the present section, we study the fibers of the direct image map
\pi_{*}
defined between the Hilbert schemes of generalized divisors of
X
and
Y
.

First, we introduce a similar notion for Weil divisors and we see how it relates to the direct image for generalized divisors.

Definition 3.2.1
.

The
direct image for Weil divisors
associated to
\pi
is the morphism of schemes:

\pi_{*}:\operatorname{WDiv}^{+}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{WDiv}^{+}(Y)

given on the level of points by

\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}\cdot[x_{i}]\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}\cdot[\pi(x_{i})].

Proposition 3.2.2
.

Assume that
Y
is smooth. There is a commutative square of schemes over
k
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{WDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{WDiv}(Y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

Proof.

Let
D
be an effective generalized divisor on
X
. Following the bottom-left side of the square, we obtain:

\pi_{*}(\rho_{X}(D))=\sum_{x\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{x}(D)\cdot[\pi(x)],

while on the top-right side of the square we have

\rho_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))\cdot[y].

Since
Y
is smooth,
\pi_{*}(D)
is Cartier locally at each point of
Y
. By Proposition
2.3.23
, for any point
y
in codimension 1 of
Y

\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))=\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D).

Then we compute:

\displaystyle\rho_{Y}(\pi_{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{y}(\pi_{*}(D))\cdot[y]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\left(\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)\right)\cdot[y]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\textrm{ cod 1}}\sum_{\pi(x)=y}\deg_{x}(D)\cdot[\pi_{*}(x)]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\textrm{ cod 1}}\deg_{x}(D)\cdot[y]=\pi_{*}(\rho_{X}(D)).

∎

We are now ready to study the fibers of the direct image for generalized divisors.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Assume that
Y
is smooth and
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities. Let
E\in\operatorname{Hilb}_{Y}
be an effective divisor of degree
d
on
Y
and let
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
be the corresponding fiber in
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
. Then,
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
is an open and non-empty dense subset of
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2.2
, there is a commutative diagram of schemes over
k
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{WDiv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{WDiv}(Y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}

First note that, since
Y
is smooth, the map
\pi_{*}
is surjective by Corollary
2.3.26
, hence
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
is non-empty. Moreover, the Hilbert-Chow morphism
\rho_{Y}
is an isomorphism. Then,

\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)=\rho_{X}^{-1}\pi_{*}^{-1}(\rho_{Y}(E)).

Let
S=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\}
be the support of
E
and let
d_{i}=\deg_{y_{i}}E
for each
i
; then

\rho_{Y}(E)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}\cdot[y_{i}].

For each
i
, denote with
\{x_{i}^{1},\dots,x_{i}^{n_{i}}\}
the discrete fiber of
\pi
over
y_{i}
. Since points can occur with multeplicity in the geometric fibers,
n_{i}\leq n
holds for each
i
. The fiber of
\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}\cdot[y_{i}]
is then the discrete subset of
\operatorname{WDiv}(X)
given by:

\pi_{*}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}\cdot[y_{i}]\right)=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}\cdot[x_{i}^{j}]\ \middle|\ c_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\ \sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}=d_{i}\textrm{ for any }i\right\}.

Denote with
Z
the set of all tuples
(c_{ij})
of positive integers with the conditions that
\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}=d_{i}
for any
i
. Then, we can write the previous set as

\pi_{*}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}\cdot[y_{i}]\right)=\bigsqcup_{(c_{ij})\in Z}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}\cdot[x_{i}^{j}]\right\}.

Then, applying
\rho_{*}^{-1}
, we otbain:

\displaystyle\rho_{X}^{-1}\pi_{*}^{-1}(\rho_{Y}(E))

\displaystyle=\rho_{X}^{-1}\pi_{*}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}\cdot[y_{i}]\right)=

\displaystyle=\rho_{X}^{-1}\left(\bigsqcup_{(c_{ij})\in Z}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}\cdot[x_{i}^{j}]\right\}\right)=

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{(c_{ij})\in Z}\rho_{X}^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{ij}\cdot[x_{i}^{j}]\right),

where the last disjoint union is a disjoint union of topological subspaces of
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}
in the same connected component. Then,
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
is a non-emtpy disjoint uniont of a finite number of fibers of the Hilbert-Chow morphism. By Proposition
3.1.3
, the intersection of any such fiber with the Cartier locus
\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
is open and dense in the same fiber. Taking the disjoint union,
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
is a non-empty open and dense subset of
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
.
∎

3.3.
The fibers of the Norm map and
\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)

In this section, assuming that
Y
is smooth and
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities, we prove that the generalized Prym of
X
with respect to
Y
is non-empty, open and dense in the Prym stack of
X
with respect to
Y
.
The theorem is based upon the following two auxiliaries proposition.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Assume that
Y
is smooth and let
\mathcal{N}\in J(Y)
be any line bundle on
Y
. Then, there is a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
X
such that
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Let
D
be a Cartier divisor on
Y
such that
\mathcal{N}\in[D]
and let
D=E-F
where
E,F
are effective Cartier divisors by Lemma
2.2.3
. By Proposition
3.2.3
there are effective Cartier divisors
H,K
on
X
such that
\pi_{*}(H)=E
and
\pi_{*}(K)=F
. Then, by Lemma
2.3.4

\pi_{*}(H-K)=D
. Set
H-K=C
and let
\mathcal{L}
be the defining ideal of
C
, seen as a line bundle. By Lemma
2.3.11
we conclude that
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{L})\simeq\mathcal{N}
.
∎

Proposition 3.3.2
.

Assume that
Y
is smooth and
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities and let
\mathcal{N}\in J(Y)
be any line bundle on
Y
. Then, the fiber
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})
is non-empty and contains
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})\cap J(Y)
as an open and dense substack.

Proof.

First, note that
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)=\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
since
X
is reduced. The fiber
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})
is non-empty by Proposition
3.3.1
and the substack
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})\cap J(Y)
is open in
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})
since being locally free is an open condition. To prove that it is dense, recall that for any fixed line bundle
M\in J(X)
there is a commutative diagram of stacks over
k
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hilb}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}}

Moreover, by
[
MRV17a
, Proposition 2.5]
there is a cover of
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
by
k
-finite type open subsets
\{U_{\beta}\}
such that, for each
\beta
, there is a line bundle
M_{\beta}\in J(X)
with the property that
\mathcal{A}_{M_{\beta}|V_{\beta}}:\mathcal{A}_{M_{\beta}}^{-1}(U_{\beta})=V_{\beta}\rightarrow U_{\beta}
is smooth and surjective.

Since density can be checked locally, fix
M=M_{\beta}
,
U=U_{\beta}
and
V=V_{\beta}
. Then, we have:

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})\cap U=\mathcal{A}_{M}\left(V\cap\pi_{*}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\right)\right)

and

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{N})\cap J(X)\cap U=\mathcal{A}_{M}\left(V\cap\pi_{*}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\right)\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}\right).

Put
\textbf{K}=\mathcal{A}_{\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(M)}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})
. Then, the topological space underlying
\pi_{*}^{-1}(\textbf{K})
contains the topological union of fibers of the points in
K
, so that:

\pi_{*}^{-1}(\textbf{K})\supseteq\bigcup_{E\in\textbf{K}}\pi_{*}^{-1}(E).

By Proposition
3.2.3
,
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
is a non-empty open and dense subscheme of
\pi_{*}^{-1}(E)
for any closed point
E\in\textbf{K}
. Hence,
\pi_{*}^{-1}(\textbf{K})\cap\operatorname{{}^{\ell}Hilb}_{X}
is non-empty, open and dense in
\pi_{*}^{-1}(\textbf{K})
. Intersecting with
V
and composing with
\mathcal{A}_{M}
, we get the thesis.
∎

We finally come to the Prym stack of
X
with respect to
Y
. Recall that by definition:

\displaystyle\Pr(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\cap J(X),

\displaystyle\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}).

Corollary 3.3.3
.

\Pr(X,Y)
is non-empty, open and dense in
\overline{\Pr}(X,Y)
.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
and apply Proposition
3.3.2
.
∎

Chapter 4
Spectral data for
G
-Higgs pairs

In this chapter we study how the spectral correspondence (see Chapter
1
, Section
1.4
) specializes for
G
-Higgs pairs, where
G=\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{PSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
. Throughout this chapter,
C
denotes a fixed smooth curve over the field of complex numbers and
L
a fixed line bundle on
C
with degree
\ell=\deg L
.

4.1.
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs

The Special Linear Group
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
is defined as:

\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})=\{M\in\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C}):\det M=1\}.

The Lie algebra associated to
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
is

\mathfrak{sl}(r,\mathbb{C})=\left\{X\in\mathfrak{gl}(r,\mathbb{C}):\operatorname{tr}X=0\right\}.

If
(P,\phi)
is a
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair on
C
, the associated vector bundle
E
is endowed with a volume form
\lambda:\det E\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{C}
and the associated Higgs field
\Phi
is a
L
-valued endomorphism with
\operatorname{tr}\Phi=0
; we say that
(E,\Phi)
is a Higgs pair
with trace zero
. Viceversa, let
(E,\Phi)
be a Higgs pair of rank
r
such that
\operatorname{tr}\Phi=0
and let
\lambda:\det E\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{C}
be a volume form on
E
; then, the frame bundle
P=\operatorname{Fr}_{\mathrm{SL}}(E,\lambda)
of all ordered basis whose
\lambda
-volume equals 1 is a principal
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-bundle, and the Higgs field
\Phi
is the image of a unique global section
\phi
of
\ad P\otimes L
with respect to the morphism
\ad P\otimes L\rightarrow\operatorname{End}E\otimes L
induced by the canonical embedding
\rho:\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
.

To sum up, the datum
(P,\phi)
of a
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair on
C
corresponds univocally, via the associated bundle construction, to the datum of
(E,\Phi,\lambda)
, where
(E,\Phi)
is a Higgs pair of rank
r
with trace zero, and
\lambda
is a trivialization of
\det E
. Note that the isomorphism
\det E\simeq\mathcal{O}_{C}
implies in particular that the Higgs pairs associated to
\mathrm{SL}
-Higgs pairs have always degree equal to 0.

A basis for the invariant polynomials of
\mathfrak{sl}(r,\mathbb{C})
is given by
\{p_{2},\dots,p_{r}\}
where
p_{i}(P,\phi):=(-)^{i}\operatorname{tr}(\wedge^{i}\phi)
; in particular, if
(E,\Phi)
is the associated Higgs pair, then
p_{i}(P,\phi)=a_{i}(E,\phi)
. Then, the
\mathrm{SL}
-Hitchin morphism of rank
r
can be defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{SL},r}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{SL}}(r):=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{SL}}(r,0)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{SL}}(r)=\bigoplus_{i=2}^{r}H^{0}(C,L^{i})

\displaystyle(P,\phi)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(p_{2}(P,\phi),\dots,p_{r}(P,\phi))=

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=(a_{2}(E,\Phi),\dots,a_{r}(E,\Phi))

\displaystyle\hskip 25.00003pt\textrm{ for }(E,\Phi)\textrm{ associated to }(P,\phi).

Proposition 4.1.1
.

(Spectral correspondence for
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
)

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{SL}}(r)
be any characteristic, let
X=X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi}C
be the associated spectral curve, and denote

B:=\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}
. The fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{SL},r}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
of the
\mathrm{SL}
-Hitchin morphism is isomorphic, via the spectral correspondence, to the fiber
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)
of the Norm map from
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
to
J(C,d^{\prime})
induced by
\pi
for
d^{\prime}=\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\ell
.

Proof.

The datum of a
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair
(P,\phi)
with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
corresponds uniquely, via the associated bundle construction, to the datum of
(E,\Phi,\lambda)
, where
(E,\Phi)
is a Higgs pair of rank
r
and degree 0 with
\mathcal{H}_{r,0}(E,\Phi)={\underline{a}}
and
\lambda
is a trivialization of
\det E
.

The datum of the Higgs pair
(E,\Phi)\in\mathcal{H}_{r,0}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
corresponds uniquely, via the spectral correspondence (Proposition
1.4.4
), to the datum of a torsion-free rank-1 sheaf
\mathcal{M}
on
X
of degree
d^{\prime}
. By Definition
2.5.1
,
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M}):=\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{M})\otimes B
, so giving a isomorphism
\lambda
between
\det\pi_{*}\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{O}_{C}
is the same as giving a isomorphism
\epsilon
between
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}(\mathcal{M})
and
B
.

To sum up, the datum of
(P,\phi)
corresponds to the datum
(\mathcal{M},\epsilon)
of an element
\mathcal{M}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
and an isomorphism
\epsilon:\operatorname{Nm}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}B
. This is an element of the fiber product stack:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}^{-1}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J(C,d^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}}

We conclude that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{SL},r}^{-1}({\underline{a}})\simeq\operatorname{Nm}^{-1}(B)
.
∎

Proposition 4.1.2
.

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{reg}}_{\mathrm{SL}}(r)
be a characteristic such that the spectral curve
X
is reduced. Then,

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{SL},r}^{-1}(a)\simeq\overline{\Pr}(X,C).

Proof.

Let
B=\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}\in J(C)
. Since
X
is reduced with locally planar singularities,
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)=\operatorname{\textit{G}\mathbb{J}}(X)
by Remark
1.2.9
and
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)
contains at least a line bundle by Proposition
3.3.2
; denote it with
N
. Tensoring by
N
in
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X)
induces then by Proposition
2.5.5
a well-defined isomorphism:

\displaystyle\overline{\Pr}(X,C)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\longmapsto\mathcal{L}\otimes N.

∎

4.2.
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs

We come now to the case of
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs. Recall first that the Projective Linear Group
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
is defined by the exact sequence:

(4.1)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\lambda\mapsto\lambda I_{r}}\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow 0.

From the exponential sequence
0\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}^{*}\rightarrow 0
we deduce that
H^{2}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}^{*})=0
; hence, the sequence
4.1
applied to the structure sheaves:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C})\rightarrow 0

yields the cohomology exact sequence

(4.2)

\displaystyle H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C}^{*})\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C})){\mathrel{\to}^{q}}H^{1}(C,\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C}))\rightarrow 0.

The sequence
4.2
, read in terms of cocycles, means that
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundles are in one-to-one correspondence with equivalence classes of
\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundles, with respect to the action on associated bundles given by tensor product of line bundles on
C
. If
P
is a
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle and
\tilde{P}
is a
\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle such that
q(\tilde{P})=P
, we say that
\tilde{P}
is a
lifting
of
P
to a
\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle.

Moreover, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{pgl}(r,\mathbb{C})
associated to
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
is equal to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(r,\mathbb{C})
of
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
. If
P
is any
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle and
\tilde{P}
is a lifting of
P
to a
\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle, then a section
\phi
of
H^{0}(C,\ad P\otimes L)
determines uniquely a section
\tilde{\phi}
of
H^{0}(C,\ad\tilde{P}\otimes L)
with trace equal to
0
, and viceversa. We say that
(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})
is a lifting of
(P,\phi)
to a
\mathrm{GL}
-Higgs pair and with a slight abuse of notation we write
q(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})=(P,\phi)
.

Tu sum up, any
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair
(P,\phi)
has a lifting
(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})
to a to a
\mathrm{GL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair, corresponding to a Higgs pair
(E,\Phi)
with trace zero via the associated bundle construction. Then, the datum of
(P,\phi)
corresponds uniquely to the datum of the equivalence class
[(E,\Phi)]
of Higgs pairs on
C
with trace zero, under the equivalence relation
\sim_{J(C)}
defined by:

(E,\Phi)\sim_{J(C)}(E\otimes N,\Phi\otimes 1_{N})\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{for any }N\in J(C).

Let
\mathcal{M}(r)=\coprod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{M}(r,d)
be the moduli stack of Higgs pairs of rank
r
in any degree and denote with
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r)
the closed substack of
\mathcal{M}(r)
given by Higgs pairs of rank
r
with trace zero. Then,
[(E,\Phi)]
is the orbit of
(E,\Phi)
under the action of
J(C)
on
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r)
defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r)\times J(C)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r)

\displaystyle((E,\Phi),N)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(E\otimes N,\Phi\otimes 1_{N}).

The degree of Higgs pairs associated to a
\mathrm{PGL}
-Higgs pair is defined only modulo
r
. Indeed, if
N
is any line bundle on
C
, then

\displaystyle\deg(E\otimes N)

\displaystyle=\deg(\det(E\otimes N))=

\displaystyle=\deg(\det(E)\otimes N^{r})=\deg(E)+r\deg(N)

so the degree of
J(C)
-equivalent Higgs pairs may differ by multiples of
r
. Hence, we can give the following definition.

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
(P,\phi)
be a
\mathrm{PGL}
-Higgs pair and let
(E,\Phi)
be a Higgs pair with trace zero and degree
d\in\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to a lifting
(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})
of
(P,\phi)
to a
\mathrm{GL}
-Higgs pair. The
degree of

(P,\phi)
is the congruence class
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Obviously, the same definition can be given just for
\mathrm{PGL}
-principal bundles. Let
P
be a
\mathrm{PGL}
-principal bundle ald let
E
be the vector bundle associated to any lifting of
P
to a
\mathrm{GL}
-principal bundle. The
degree
of a
\mathrm{PGL}
-principal bundles
P
is the congruence class
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
of the degree
d=\deg E
.

As a matter of fact, the first homotopy group
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C}))
of
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
and the degree of
(P,\phi)
characterizes uniquely the topological type of
P
.

Up to the action on
(E,\Phi)
of a line bundle of degree
1
on
C
, it is straightforward to see that the
J(C)
-orbit
[(E,\Phi)]
corresponding to a
\mathrm{PGL}
-Higgs pair with degree
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
is always the orbit of a Higgs pair of degree
d
with
d\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
. If we restrict the correspondence to Higgs pairs with fixed degree
d\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
, then a
\mathrm{PGL}
-Higgs pair of degree
\overline{d}
is identified uniquely with the orbit of a Higgs pair of trace zero
and degree
d
with respect to the action of line bundles
of degree

0
on
C
. We have then the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
with
d\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
. Denote with
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r,d)
the closed substack of
\mathcal{M}(r,d)
given by Higgs pairs with trace zero. The moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r,\overline{d})
of
\mathrm{PGL}
-Higgs pairs of rank
r
and degree
\overline{d}
is isomorphic to the quotient stack

\displaystyle q:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r,d)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r,d)/J^{0}(C)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r,\overline{d})

\displaystyle(E,\Phi)

\displaystyle\longmapsto[(E,\Phi)]\longmapsto(P,\phi):=q(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})

where
(E,\Phi)
is associated to the
\mathrm{GL}
-principal bundle
(\tilde{P},\tilde{\phi})
.

A basis for the invariant polynomials of
\mathfrak{pgl}(r,\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{sl}(r,\mathbb{C})
is given by
p_{2},\dots,p_{r}
where
p_{i}(P,\phi):=(-)^{i}\operatorname{tr}(\wedge^{i}\phi)
; moreover, if
[(E,\Phi)]
is the associated
J^{0}(C)
-equivalence class of Higgs pairs with zero trace, then
p_{i}(P,\phi)=a_{i}(E,\Phi)
for any
(E,\Phi)
associated to a lifting of
(P,\phi)
. Hence, we have the following
\mathrm{PGL}
-Hitchin morphism of rank
r
and degree
\overline{d}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{PGL},r,\overline{d}}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r,\overline{d})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r)=\bigoplus_{i=2}^{r}H^{0}(C,L^{i})

\displaystyle(P,\phi)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(p_{2}(P,\phi),\dots,p_{r}(P,\phi)).

The
\mathrm{PGL}
-Hitchin morphism fits the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}(r,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r,d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r,\overline{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=2}^{r}H^{0}(C,L^{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{r,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{r,d}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{PGL},r,\overline{d}}}

with
d\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
and
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
.

We are now ready to state the spectral correspondence for
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

(Spectral correspondence for
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
)

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r)
be any characteristic and let
X=X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi}C
be the associated spectral curve. Let
\overline{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
with
d\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
be any degree. The fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{PGL},r,\overline{d}}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
of the
\mathrm{PGL}
-Hitchin morphism is isomorphic, via the spectral correspondence, to the quotient moduli space

\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})/\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)

of torsion-free sheaves of rank 1 and degree
d^{\prime}
up to the action of line bundles of degree
0
on
C
by tensor product, with
d^{\prime}=d+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\ell
.

Proof.

The datum of a
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair
(P,\phi)
with degree
\overline{d}
and characteristic
{\underline{a}}
corresponds uniquely to the datum of a
J^{0}(C)
-equivalence class
[(E,\Phi)]
of Higgs pairs of rank
r
and degree
d
with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
, where
(E,\Phi)
is the Higgs pair associated, via the vector bundle construction, to a lifting of
(P,\phi)
to a
\mathrm{GL}
-Higgs pair. By the spectral correspondence, the datum of
(E,\Phi)
corresponds uniquely to the datum of a pure sheaf
\mathcal{M}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
such that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{M}=E
and
d^{\prime}=d+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\ell
. Let
N\in J^{0}(C)
be any line bundle of degree 0 on
C
. By the projection formula,

\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}N)\simeq\pi_{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes N,

and the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}N)\otimes L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes N)\otimes L.}
∼
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\cdot x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{N}}
∼

Hence, the spectral correspondence is equivariant with respect to the action of
\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)
by tensor product on
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
and the action of
J^{0}(C)
on
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}(r,d)
. Then, passing to the quotient on both sides, the datum of the
J^{0}(C)
-orbit
[(E,\Phi)]
corresponds uniquely to the datum of a
\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)
-orbit
[\mathcal{M}]\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})/\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)
.
∎

We study now the special case of
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs of degree
0
. Since
\mathbb{C}
is algebraically closed, the Projective Linear group is equal to the Projective Special Linear group
\mathrm{PSL}(r,\mathbb{C})
defined by the exact sequence:

(4.3)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mu_{r}\xrightarrow{\lambda\mapsto\lambda I_{r}}\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}(r,\mathbb{C})\rightarrow 0;

here we denote with
\mu_{r}
the group of
r
-th roots of unity, as defined by the exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mu_{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{r}}\mathbb{C}^{*}\rightarrow 0.

In particular,
H^{2}(C,\mu_{r})=\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
, hence the exact sequence
4.3
, applied to structure sheaves, induces the cohomology exact sequence:

(4.4)

\displaystyle H^{1}(C,\mu_{r})\rightarrow H^{1}(C,\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C})){\mathrel{\to}^{q}}H^{1}(C,\mathrm{PSL}(r,\mathcal{O}_{C}))\xrightarrow{\deg}\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow 0.

The sequence
4.4
, read in terms of cocycles, means that a
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle
P
can be lifted to a principal
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle
P_{0}
if and only if
P
has degree
0\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
; any other lifting
P_{0}^{\prime}
of
P
differs from
P_{0}
by the action on associated bundles of a
r
-th torsion line bundle by tensor product. Moreover, a Higgs field
\phi
on
P
determines uniquely a Higgs field
\phi_{0}
on
P_{0}
, and viceversa.

To sum up, any
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle
(P,\phi)
of degree zero has a lifting
(P_{0},\phi_{0})
to a
\mathrm{SL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle, corresponding to the datum
(E,\Phi,\lambda)
of a Higgs pair
(E,\Phi)
with trace zero and a volume form
\lambda:\det E\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{C}
via the associated bundle construction. Then, the datum of
(P,\phi)
corresponds uniquely to the datum of the equivalence class
[(E,\phi,\lambda)]
of Higgs pairs with trace zero, under the equivalence relation
\sim_{J^{0}(C)[r]}
defined by:

(E,\Phi,\lambda)\sim_{J^{0}(C)[r]}(E\otimes N,\Phi\otimes 1_{N},\lambda_{N,\epsilon})\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{for any }(N,\epsilon)\in J^{0}(C)[r].

Here,
J^{0}(C)[r]
is the group stack of
r
-torsion line bundles
(N,\eta)
on
C
, where
\eta
is the isomorphism
N^{r}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{O}_{C}
, and
\lambda_{N,\eta}
denote the isomorphism:

\lambda_{N,\eta}:\det(E\otimes N)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\det(E)\otimes N^{r}\xrightarrow[\lambda\otimes\eta]{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{C}.

We can prove now the following proposition.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

(Spectral correspondence for
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
of degree 0)

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{PGL}}(r)
be any characteristic, let
X=X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi}C
be the associated spectral curve and denote

B:=\det(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})^{-1}
. Let
d^{\prime}=\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\ell
and let
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}
be the Norm map induced by
\pi
on
\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
. Let
J^{0}(C)[r]
be the group stack of line bundles with
r
-th torsion on
C
, acting on
\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)
as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)\times\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)[r]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)

\displaystyle((\mathcal{M},\epsilon),(\pi^{*}N,\pi^{*}\eta))

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}N,\epsilon_{N,\eta})

where
\epsilon_{N,\eta}
is equal to the following composition:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N,\eta}:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nm}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}N)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\operatorname{Nm}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\operatorname{Nm}(\pi^{*}N)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\operatorname{Nm}(\mathcal{M})\otimes N^{r}\xrightarrow{\epsilon\otimes\eta}B.

Then, the fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{PGL},r,\overline{0}}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
of the
\mathrm{PGL}
-Hitchin morphism is isomorphic, via the spectral correspondence, to the quotient moduli space

\operatorname{Nm}_{\pi}^{-1}(B)/\pi^{*}(J^{0}C)[r].

Proof.

The datum of a
\mathrm{PGL}(r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair
(P,\phi)
with degree
0
and characteristic
{\underline{a}}
corresponds uniquely to the datum of a
J^{0}(C)[r]
-equivalence class
[(E,\Phi,\lambda)]
of Higgs pairs of rank
r
and degree
0
with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
, where
(E,\Phi,\lambda)
is the datum of a Higgs pair with a volume form
\lambda
associated, via the vector bundle construction, to a lifting of
(P,\phi)
to a
\mathrm{SL}
-Higgs pair.

By Proposition
4.1.1
, the datum of
(E,\Phi,\lambda)
corresponds to the datum
(M,\epsilon)
of a torsion-free rank-1 sheaf
\mathcal{M}
of degree
d^{\prime}
on
X
and an isomorphism
\epsilon:\operatorname{Nm}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}B
, i.e. an element of
\operatorname{Nm}^{-1}(B)
. If
(N,\eta)
is a line bundle on
C
with
r
-th torsion, the datum of
(E\otimes N,\Phi_{N},\lambda_{N,\eta})
corresponds by projection formula to the datum of
(M\otimes\pi^{*}N,\epsilon_{N,\eta)}
. Then, the datum of the
J^{0}(C)[r]
-equivalence class
[(E,\Phi,\lambda)]
corresponds uniquely to the datum of the
\pi^{*}J^{0}(C)[r]
-equivalence class
[(\mathcal{M},\epsilon)]
.

∎

4.3.
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs

The symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
is defined as:

\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})=\{M\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,\mathbb{C}):M\Omega M^{t}=\Omega\}

where
\Omega=\left(\begin{matrix}0&I_{r}\\
-I_{r}&0\end{matrix}\right)
.

The Lie algebra associated to
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{X\in\mathfrak{gl}(2r,\mathbb{C}):X\Omega+\Omega X^{t}=0\right\}.

If
(P,\phi)
is a
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair on
C
, the associated vector bundle
E
is endowed with a non-degenerate symplectic form
\omega:E\otimes E\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}
and the associated Higgs field
\Phi
is satisfies the condition
\omega(\Phi v,w)=-\omega(v,\Phi w)
for all sections
v,w
of
E
. The existence of a non-degenerate symplectic form implies that
E
has trivial determinant and, in particular, that
E
has degree
0
. Viceversa, let
(E,\Phi)
be a Higgs pair of rank
2r
and let
\omega
be a non-degenerate symplectic form on
E
satisfying the condition
\omega(\Phi v,w)=-\omega(v,\Phi w)
; then, the frame bundle
P=\operatorname{Fr}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(E,\omega)
of all ordered symplectic basis of
(E,\omega)
is a principal
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-bundle, and the Higgs field
\Phi
is the image of a unique global section
\phi
of
\ad P\otimes L
with respect to the morphism
\ad P\otimes L\rightarrow\operatorname{End}E\otimes L
induced by the canonical embedding
\rho:\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(2r,\mathbb{C})
.

To sum up, the datum of a
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair
(P,\phi)
on
C
corresponds univocally to the datum of
(E,\Phi,\omega)
where
(E,\Phi)
is a Higgs pair of rank
2r
and degree
0
and
\omega:E\otimes E\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}
is a non-degenerate symplectic form on
E
satisfying the condition:

\omega(\Phi v,w)=-\omega(v,\Phi w).

A basis for the invariant polynomials of
\mathfrak{sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
is given by
\{p_{2i}\}_{i=1,\dots,r}
where
p_{2i}(P,\phi):=\operatorname{tr}(\wedge^{2i}\phi)
; if
(E,\Phi)
is the associated Higgs pair, then
p_{2i}(P,\phi)=a_{2i}(E,\phi)
. The corresponding
\mathrm{Sp}
-Hitchin morphism takes the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sp},2r}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(2r):=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(2r,0)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(2r)=\bigoplus_{l=1}^{r}H^{0}(C,L^{2l})

\displaystyle(P,\phi)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(p_{2}(P,\phi),p_{4}(P,\phi),\dots,p_{2r}(P,\phi)).

For any characteristic
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(2r)
, the spectral curve
\pi:X_{\underline{a}}\rightarrow C
is defined in the total space
p:\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{C}\oplus L^{-1})\rightarrow C
by the equation

x^{2r}y+a_{2}x^{2r-2}y^{2}+\dots+a_{2r-2}x^{2}y^{2r-2}+a_{2r}y^{2r}=0.

Hence, the curve
X=X_{\underline{a}}
has an involution
\sigma
defined by
\sigma(x)=-x
and a quotient curve:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/\sigma=Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}}

The involution
\sigma
induces by pullback an involution on the compactified Jacobian of torsion-free rank-1 sheaves with any degree
d^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}:\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sigma^{*}\mathcal{L}.

Proposition 4.3.1
.

(Spectral correspondence for
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
)

Let
{\underline{a}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(2r)
be any characteristic and let
X=X_{\underline{a}}\xrightarrow{\pi}C
be the associated spectral curve with involution
\sigma:X\rightarrow X
. The fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sp},2r}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
of the
\mathrm{Sp}
-Hitchin morphism is isomorphic, via the spectral correspondence, to the equalizer
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{a}}
of the two maps

\displaystyle\texttt{\char 95\relax}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}:=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\texttt{\char 95\relax},\mathcal{O}_{X}):\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,-d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}\texttt{\char 95\relax}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}:\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,-d^{\prime}),

where
d^{\prime}=r(2r-1)\ell
.

Proof.

The dual of a torsion-free sheaf on a Gorenstein curve is torsion-free sheaf with the same rank, hence the map
\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\texttt{\char 95\relax},\mathcal{O}_{X})
is well-defined. Let
(P,\phi)
be any
\mathrm{Sp}
-Higgs pair with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
and let
(E,\Phi,\omega)
be the associated datum of a Higgs pair of rank
2r
and degree 0 with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
and symplectic form
\omega
. The torsion-free sheaf
\mathcal{M}
on
X
associated to
(E,\Phi)
by the spectral correspondence fits into the exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}\rightarrow\pi^{*}E\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}\Phi-x}\pi^{*}(E\otimes L)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L\rightarrow 0.

Taking the dualized sequence and tensoring by
\pi^{*}L
gives a left exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\rightarrow\pi^{*}E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}\Phi^{t}-x}\pi^{*}E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\otimes\pi^{*}L.

On the other hand, applying
\sigma^{*}
to the first exact sequence gives:

0\rightarrow\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}\rightarrow\pi^{*}E\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}\Phi+x}\pi^{*}(E\otimes L)\rightarrow\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L\rightarrow 0.

Now, the symplectic form
\omega
induce an isomorphism
\omega_{E}:E\simeq E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}
and hence a commutative diagram with vertical isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\otimes\pi^{*}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}(E\otimes L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\Phi^{t}-x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\pi^{*}\Phi-x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Hence, we conclude that the sheaf
\mathcal{M}
comes with a sheaf isomorphism

\lambda:\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}.

Viceversa, suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a torsion-free sheaf of rank 1 and degree
d^{\prime}
on
X
with an isomorphism
\lambda:\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}
. Let
(E,\Phi)
be the
\mathrm{GL}
-Higgs pair with characteristic
{\underline{a}}
corresponding to
\mathcal{M}
. By
[
HS93
]
, the Relative duality formula holds for
X/C
with dualizing sheaf equal to the sheaf of relative differentials
\omega_{X/C}
. By Lemma
1.4.1
and Riemann-Hurwitz we obtain that
\omega_{X/C}\simeq\pi^{*}L^{2r-1}
. Applying Relative duality in dimension 0 and the projection formula, we obtain an isomorphism:

\displaystyle E^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M})^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\simeq\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{2r-1})\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{*}(\sigma^{*}(\mathcal{M}))=\pi_{*}(\sigma_{*}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M})\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{*}(\sigma_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{M})=\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{M})\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{*}(\mathcal{M})=E;

that induces the symplectic structure
\omega:E\otimes E\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

To sum up, the datum of
(P,\phi)
in the fiber
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Sp},2r}^{-1}({\underline{a}})
corresponds uniquely to the datum
(\mathcal{M},\lambda)
of a torsion-free rank-1 sheaf
\mathcal{M}\in\overline{\mathbb{J}}(X,d^{\prime})
and an isomorphism
\lambda:\mathcal{M}^{\smash{\scalebox{.7}[1.4]{\rotatebox{90.0}{{\T1\guilsinglleft}}}}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\sigma^{*}\mathcal{M}\otimes\pi^{*}L^{1-2r}
, i.e. an element of the equalizer stack
\mathcal{E}_{\underline{a}}
.
∎

4.4.
\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pairs

The General Symplectic group
\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
is defined as:

\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})=\{M\in\mathrm{GL}(2r,\mathbb{C}):M\Omega M^{t}=\lambda\Omega\textrm{ for some }\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\}

where
\Omega=\left(\begin{matrix}0&I_{r}\\
-I_{r}&0\end{matrix}\right)
.

The Lie algebra associated to
\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{gsp}(2r,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{A\in\mathfrak{gl}(2r,\mathbb{C}):A\Omega+\Omega A^{t}=\frac{\operatorname{tr}A}{r}\Omega\right\}\simeq

\displaystyle\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})\oplus\mathbb{C}

where the isomorphism is given by the decomposition
A=X+\frac{\operatorname{tr}A}{2r}I_{2r}
, with
X\in\mathfrak{sp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
and
\operatorname{tr}(A)\in\mathbb{C}
.

If
(P,\phi)
is a
\mathrm{GSp}(2r,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs pair on
C
, the associated vector bundle
E
is endowed with a non-degenerate symplectic form
\omega:E\otimes E\rightarrow M
with values in a line bundle
M
on
C
and the associated Higgs field
\Phi
satisfies the condition

\omega(\Phi v,w)+\omega(v,\Phi w)=\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Phi}{r}\omega(v,w)

for all sections
v,w
of
E
. The existence of a non-degenerate symplectic
M
-valued form implies that
E
has determinant isomorphic to
M^{r}
and, in particular, that
E
has degree equal to
r\deg M
. Viceversa, let
(E,\Phi)
be a Higgs pair of rank
2r
and let
\omega
be a non-degenerate symplectic
M
-valued form on
E
satisfying the condition

\omega(\Phi v,w)+\omega(v,\Phi w)=\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Phi}{r}\omega(v,w);

then, the frame bundle
